[p. I]

[p. III] To the

Berlin Academy of Sciences,

to Messrs

Herder, Schlosser, Eberhard,

to each

a rescuer of the ailing understanding

of the schools in Germany, and therefore especially also to

Mr

Friedrich Nicolai,

this monument is dedicated

by German love of the fatherland.

[p. V]

## Preface.

In the preface to my essay on the *Origin of the Concept of Freedom of the Will* (Stuttgart 1796), pp. V, VI, I declared that “*nothing would keep me from first seeking out man everywhere in experience and history, then tracing back to natural laws what is in him, as exactly as if there were nothing else in him, and finally, after this natural-historical* [p. VI] *and physical manner of treating him, from seeing with my own eyes what, of its possessions, then still falls to metaphysics.*” This resolve has now been carried out. Turned away with my further investigations by a sated empiricism, I turned to higher speculation, and thus to the *doctrine of reason*. A fitting occasion for this was offered me, during the last Easter holidays, by a short stay in the country, whither *Bilfinger’s* and *Ploucquet’s* logics accompanied me, since the other company of my idle hours, the philosophers of Greece, could not well follow me there in any case. Here are the results of my reflection, as the inaugural essay of their author in that domain of more abstract inquiry. Whether the, to them [p. VII] prefixed title is presumptuous, its content must teach; the preface can spare itself that. Immodesty has, at least, never yet been made a reproach to me. Rather I recall having found it publicly remarked, more than once, and only quite recently again, that the bounds of decorum and moderation, even in dealings with opponents, are nowhere found overstepped by me. It is therefore solely to itself and its own character that one must unfailingly ascribe, partly the evil that was to be met, partly the conduct of its authors toward those who think otherwise, if a writer who was never accustomed to such a thing otherwise allows himself a stronger language against it. As circumstances stand, this is unavoidable. Whoever undertakes it prepares himself for the disagreeable consequences [p. VIII] that immediately follow; but he also consoles himself over this with the presentiment of a later gratitude. *O magna vis veritatis, quae contra hominum ingenia, calliditatem, solertiam, contraque fictas omnium insidias facile se per se ipsam defendat* [O great power of truth, that easily defends itself by itself against the wits of men, their cunning, their cleverness, and against the feigned snares of all!]. Where it has once come to this in a sect, that whoever is not of its faith is dismissed with the honorary title of an *uncritical ignoramus*, that it is not ashamed to declare every product that does not bear its stamp a schoolboyish exercise-piece, that schoolboyish youths from its midst even determine, exclusively and publicly, a man’s fitness for any philosophical purpose whatever by his share in their kinship, then it seems that it has itself proclaimed all delicacy to be at an end. If, moreover, the present writing becomes bitter, [p. IX] it will be so never against merit, but against youths of the rabble just described, who, turned giddy by untimely praise and strengthened in their conceit by weak heads, with a wisdom borrowed from the *Königsberg man*, but, as they fancied, far more than *Kantian*, wanted to become *tone-setting* for Germany’s philosophy, whereas the rod of discipline would still have been due to them. Speculation (like politicizing, if it is not to be a pewterer’s politics) requires *ripeness*, and no youth is ripe, and ripe not all men can become:

*Cedo, qui vestram rempublicam tantam amisistis tam cito? Proveniebant oratores novi, stulti, adolescentuli.* [Tell me, how did you lose so great a state so quickly? New orators kept turning up, foolish young men.]

Otherwise I know nothing to add here, except perhaps this, that if, in the pursuit of [p. X] my *prius*, the consequence led in part to *Leibnizian* representations, it was this consequence, and not *Leibniz* himself. It must do this great man more honor to be found by me also where one did not look for him, than merely to pay homage to him because one had already set out from him in one’s investigations.

Stuttgart, the 18th of Aug. 1799.

[p. XI]

## P. S.

In the Intelligence Sheet of the Jena *Allgemeine Literatur-Zeitung*, No. 109, Wednesday 28 Aug. 1799, p. 876, it says, on the occasion of the declaration in which Mr *Kant* drops *Fichte’s Doctrine of Science as an entirely untenable system*, among other things: “for pure doctrine of science is nothing more or less than *mere logic*, which with its principles does not climb up to the material element of cognition, but as *pure logic* abstracts from its content; to pick a *real object* out of it is a futile labor, *and therefore one never yet attempted*.” If, then, it really is a labor *never yet attempted* to posit a real object through *pure logic* (demonstratively), and the, in the previous logics [p. XII] noticed errors force upon one whom empiricism could no longer satisfy, in his sufficiently prepared transition to higher speculation, the thought that it is for logic itself still to constitute its *prius*, that it itself has hitherto been merely a heteronomous concrescence of material and form, even in its supposedly pure laws of thought; and, as he, accordingly, merely undertakes to provide it with its *prius*, he finds, what he had not set out for, a real object either posited *through it*, or else none positable anywhere, the key to the essence of nature either given *through it*, or else no logic and no philosophy possible at all: would this writer then be presumptuous, in case he calls his logic an Outline of *First* Logic? — It is surely a labor *never yet attempted*. — But as for the [p. XIII] *real object*, let one but once hold the following question calmly and without prejudice up to one’s own thinking, by considering whether any universality and strict necessity — be it even, for the moment, only that of time and space — would be what it nevertheless gives itself out to be in the thinking of each, namely this very universality and strict necessity, if it were something merely *subjective*, possibly belonging merely to this human species of the third planet, which rolls around the sun, and to its individual (or communal?) I-hood? — But let one likewise do logic the small service, in the second place, of holding this calmly and without prejudice up to one’s thinking: whether even *a single one* of the concepts, *one* of the judgments, *one* of the syllogisms, into whose interior it promises to introduce us, *could* take place, [p. XIV] if the *essentialia constitutiva* which it seeks to record of those concepts, judgments, syllogisms, already *as essentialia constitutiva of the same*, were to be entirely and altogether different from the *essentialia constitutiva* of that about which judgment is passed, as *essentialia constitutiva* of the latter? Whoever in this way could take up into the identity of a judgment things that in their *essence* are nothing less than identical with the *essence* of a judgment, but in their essence are rather diametrically opposed to the essence of a judgment, would have to be made for turning impossibilities into possibilities, or, which is one, in order to bring a connection nonetheless into this *fundamentally essential lack of a connection*, a *Fichtean I* would come to his aid. If logic [p. XV] is no key to the essence of nature: then the means of connection of which it is so proud are only *privative* means of connection; nature has none, or else its own logic, and the world is no system. In any case, *Epicurus* already gave the latter the deserved rebuke; aimless and planless, as in his school, its children, the atoms, embrace one another, in order aimlessly and planlessly to repel one another again; chance and caprice (a nonentity) is called its supreme law, and even there where one thinks to dispose of this chaos by means of one’s I, to the order of the day, it is the call of caprice to caprice, hence, according to Mr *Kant’s* newest language, l. c., *a, in the choice of means to favor purposes (in the universe), perversely behaving, clumsy call*, with which one spoils the time. *Universality and strict necessity cannot first come to be (γίνεσθαι) in us children of man, as out of moistures of the earth, in the plants, plant-saps* [p. XVI] *come to be*; there no chemical and no organic process suffices any longer, there is nothing *subjective* in it any more, as an Epicurus (*in altera philosophiae parte, quae* λογική *dicitur, plane inermis et nudus* [in the other part of philosophy, which is called logic, quite unarmed and naked]), *sacrificing all philosophy to the senses*, or the sophist *Protagoras*, *mocking all philosophy*, might maintain. Though it be called ten times over *human race*, what thus bears the form and essence of things privatively (subjectively) on itself, and consequently is to make everything solely into *human form*: everything would still cling to man only *as man*, and by the appendage of the *race* (for the purpose of universality) one by no means critiques oneself, with one’s new Critique, past the old one of the said sophist, which went: πάντων χρημάτων μέτρον εἶναι ἄνθρωπον. —

Stuttgart, the 17th of Sept. 1799.

[p. 1]

### §. 1.

Whoever reckons, thinks. But he thinks without describing anything other than his thinking itself, in thinking. Only in *calculating* does he describe his thinking in an object outside it.

### §. 2.

Whoever reckons *without calculating* thinks nothing besides expressing the act of his thinking itself, and yet he thinks; hence he describes the act of his thinking itself as refined and pure as possible.

*Rem.* Whoever represents numerals to himself as objects does not reckon, as long as he does not give up representing them *as certain objects*, and does not try out on them, as mere mediators of it (without otherwise meaning anything), the act of his thinking itself. *Kant* holds numbers [p. 2] for *concepts*; thus presupposes, according to his *own* system, categories and intuitions for the possibility of a *number-concept*. (*Critique of Pure Reason*, 2nd ed., p. 15 ff.) Is it possible? I ask here only provisionally. Would they not, according to his *own* system, rather have to be *ideas* — perhaps *particular* ideas in a *pure* intuition, or, to put it properly, ideas on millions, millions of packthreads of an *a priori synthesis*? Yet that is how it must go, when one takes *thinking* for a *judging* (number-concepts, number-subjects, number-predicates, number-judgments? — No, *number-formulas*, l. c. p. 105, 206), and with *pure reason*, that is to say, with *thinking*, only comes afterwards.

### §. 3.

The absolute possibility of *reckoning* rests on this, that one can repeat *one, as one and the selfsame*, infinitely many times in *the many*;

*Rem.* — consequently not on the *empirical* counter-image of a *pure* intuition, as it is yielded by the five fingers at *Königsberg*, l. c. p. 15.

[p. 3]

### §. 4.

The absolute possibility of thinking rests on this, that we can repeat *one, as one and the selfsame*, infinitely many times in *the many* (not the manifold).

### §. 5.

One must first be able to do this in *the many*, i.e. in the indeterminability of all possible cases, in order to be capable of the very same also in the *manifold*, i.e. in order to be capable (through thinking) of a *cognition*. The consideration of the former is attended to too little nowadays. One is too quick, and for that very reason all too manifold. One synthesizes, e.g., at once, in order then to be able to measure everything that comes, in a new way, by the ell.

*Rem.* Multiplicity, *as multiplicity*, cancels the continuation of one and the selfsame repetition; *plurality* posits it.

### §. 6.

That *one* is *the unchangeable* under all change, that which is *perfectly determined* through itself as well as *becoming-determining* in the indeterminable multitude of all possible cases of its use; (*Ens unum, verum, bonum s. perfectum*.) Only [p. 4] allow me the request not to concresce my one = A forthwith in phantasy with a C, hence to leave the *ens* aside, *at least in the meantime*, — the I *for ever*, —

### §. 7.

To be able to repeat A, as unity, in A, A, A etc., hence (already in this case) in *the many*, or in the indeterminability of all possible cases of its use, infinitely many times, we therefore call our thinking.

### §. 8.

To be able to posit A, with its infinite repeatability, also in C, we call: *to comprehend* C through A, or *to cognize*.

*Rem.* What A may bring with it for the C, the 6th § says, *so far as it now belongs here*, namely no *concrescing* according to its nature described there, but a *determining*. The former may be done by phantasy, which we can very well do without here.

### §. 9.

To demonstrate the comprehensibility of C, hence the *possibility* of a cognition in general, from A, is nowadays called *critique*.

[p. 5] *Rem.* The *plus* which was found still to be required for A in this respect, I set = x.

### §. 10.

To demonstrate the fundamentally essential components (*essentialia constitutiva*) of thinking from the possibility of being able to repeat A, as one, which remains the same also in the many etc. (§. 6), infinitely many times, is called *doctrine of reason*.

*Rem.* The *plus* which a *logic* requires, see below. Instead of *fundamentally essential components*, I shall in what follows, not without reason, put *ineradicable modi*, and divide these again into the *modus generalis* and the *modi speciales*; the *eradicable modi*, or mere *affections*, however, I shall oppose to those in their proper place.

### §. 11.

A, *designating the one*, which, equal to itself, unchangeable under all change, and infinitely repeatable as A, constitutes the essence of our thinking, can never become unequal to itself, never changeable under the change, never non-repeatable as A, hence A (in *this* meaning) never *Non A*.

[p. 6]

### §. 12.

*Thinking, as thinking, therefore suffers no difference of quality. Either thinking, or ceasing to think, i.e. contradicting oneself; otherwise there is in thinking, as thinking, no negation.*

*Rem.* That this *ceasing to think* is each time to be understood of the *omitted connecting* of certain representations in a thinking, and that *thinking, as thinking*, cannot cease, it will be superfluous to add here.

*Observation.*

What the 12th § states can also be inferred from this, that what is to be *a thought, no* contradiction, must be positable in thinking in some context of representations.

Corollaries from §. 12.

1) Negation can never be drawn to that which, in a *categorical* judgment, is A, hence never to the *Est*, as the *logical copula* of a categorical judgment, as nevertheless happens in all logics; for that means to declare thinking in a categorical judgment to be a not-thinking, i.e. a contradiction. *The copula has no difference of quality.*

[p. 7] *Rem.* A pity that one does not dispute publicly in mere categorical judgments too, as one does in syllogisms; otherwise it might perhaps have occurred to someone sooner that one could say just as little: *negatur copula*, as: *negatur conclusio*. The latter is laughed at; and the former is taught.

2) Negation can never be drawn to the copula in a *hypothetical* judgment, i.e. to the *consequence*; hence one can say just as little: *negatur consequentia*, as: *negatur conclusio*. *The consequence has no difference of quality as copula.*

*Rem.* A second *quid pro quo* of all logics, and at the same time an evident contradiction with their assertion that the ground necessarily posits its consequence! It is not the inference (consequence, *as copula*) that can be denied, but the sufficiency of the *matter* lying in the antecedent (*sufficientia conditionis*) to point out a ground here in thinking (to qualify itself for the copula for *this* matter, to qualify itself for a “so”). Further, A remains A here too, whether the matter may now fall in *modum ponentem* [p. 8] or *tollentem*. In both cases the copula remains unalterable, and suffers no difference of quality. That of hypothetical judgments, *as such*, it can never be said that they are affirmative or negative, that consequently with them, *as hypothetical*, all difference of quality falls away, *Maaß* too asserts (in his *Outline of Logic*, p. 143); but he seeks the ground of this not where it is, in the *unalterability of the copula*; but rather in this, that in no hypothetical judgment is the relation of a predicate to the subject thought. But what else *could* then be thought in it? — As for *Kant*, he has already, by the opening of his table of categories with *logical* quantity, promised not much that is profitable for logic. Had he begun with *quality*, he would at least still have betrayed some acquaintance with the nature of the object; for *this* first brings a difference of quality with it. But a *difference of quantity*, in the hitherto customary *logical* sense of the word, neither *thinking, as thinking*, nor the *object, as object*, knows; and with the *difference of quantity*, in the hitherto customary [p. 9] *logical* sense of the word, there begins nonetheless in *Kant* — the table of *our forms of thought*.

3) Negation can never be drawn to the copula in a *disjunctive* judgment, i.e. to the disjunction itself, *insofar as it becomes possible through a thinking*, (to the A, to the thought in the either-or, *as thought*). *The disjunction has no difference of quality as copula.*

*Rem.* Even the *Kantians* deny that disjunctive judgments can have another quality than the *affirmative*; (as if in *thinking, as thinking*, an affirmation were already possible), — although they likewise prove their assertion by a *vitium non causae pro causa*. The ground does not lie in the exhaustive character of the marks, *as marks*. How can *marks, as marks*, according to the Kantians’ *own* principles ever become exhaustive, whether one puts them together under an *either-or*, or, as one pleases. The ground (not merely the *conditio sine qua non*) lies rather in that which in the *either-or* is merely *thought*, copula, — *pure work of reason* [p. 10] is, one might say in their language; hence there is here *properly no possibility of a difference of quality, no quality at all, to be thought*, as *Maaß* too remarks; yet, as could not be otherwise, still *per substitutionem falsi medii* (page 154).

4) Negation can never be drawn to the copula in a *syllogism*, i.e. to the *conclusion* (to the Therefore, *Ergo*, as to the A in a syllogism). This was admitted long ago (*vel quasi tacite*). But where does one find the *Why*? discussed. It belonged to the logical secrets: *The conclusion has no difference of quality as copula.*

*Rem.* The question is natural, and one can therefore demand its answer: how it comes about that one here already so early took the *negating* (i.e. annihilating) of the copula for contrary to reason (did not see into it)? Answer: because among all modes of thinking a syllogism connects the most matter; and one consequently had so many accessory works of matter in which one could tumble about as a great logician, that the *Ergo*, hence the form of a syllogism as syllogism, in so far [p. 11] as a thinking is involved in it, did not come into consideration, to their good fortune; but whoever, with his oppositions, had wanted to venture even so far as the *Ergo*, by virtue of a dull feeling of the inconvenience which one believed one had to impute to him himself already as a *rational being*, would have been downright laughed at. Let one only recall what the figures of the syllogisms alone could give one by way of entertainment, before *Kant* ground them to pieces.

5) What is *merely thought* in a concept is its repeatability infinitely many times as A, which excludes every Non-A in the repetition, its copula. What is merely thought in a concept can never be negated without annihilating it as thought, i.e. without contradiction. A concept, *as thought*, tolerates no difference of quality.

*Rem.* It is clear, without my reminding, that here the copula of No. 1, the *categorical* copula, is meant; and that we have consequently to do only with *four* numbers; the fifth is merely an *opus supererogationis* for those who might have stumbled at the first, and for whom I add here that the *thought* in a categorical judgment and the *thought* in a concept are perfectly one.

[p. 12]

### Consequences of the Corollaries drawn from §. 12.

a) In *thinking, as thinking*, it is perfectly one whether *Est bonus*, *Est non bonus*, or *Non-homo est* [is good, is not good, or is a non-man]; whether He is, He was, or He will be; He is not, He was not, or He will not be, is to be represented therein. This is evident, (to put the remark on the 12th §. into German otherwise) also from the fact that circumstances can command one to let the One pass over into the Other. Only against this can circumstances do nothing, that in the infinitely repeated repeatability of the Bonus, of the Non-bonus, or Non-homo, etc., posited through *thinking, as thinking*, a non-repetition too might slip in through an altered position of things.

*Rem.* In the sentence: *He is not*, etc., it will be said, the negation is after all drawn to the copula; for where else is one to put it?—I beg for a little patience, and remind you provisionally to keep merely to the German: *He is not*: in which the positing of a something, *precisely thereby*, that one designates its mere *present* absence, may be more striking than in the *non est*. One gains much already if one keeps to the *German* [p. 13] *language*, as our forefathers handed it down to us; this is proved among other things by the word *Begriff* [concept], which, as far as I know, we alone among Europe’s cultured peoples have not filched from the Romans; in Roman it would be called *complexio*. *Les langues sont le miroir de l’esprit humain* [Languages are the mirror of the human mind], *Leibniz*.

b) What hitherto in the logics was regarded as the most insignificant thing in a categorical judgment, with which one believed one could drive the Yes or No at pleasure, and which *as thought* (for as not-thought one surely did not want to take the copula, or did one?)—hence *as mere thought*, already excluded the possibility of a Yes or No,—the *copula*—was precisely *thinking* itself, as one now says, the *Pure Reason*. In the negation of the copula, then, every logician hitherto would have negated *pure reason* to himself and to others; (*Kiesewetter’s Compendium of a General Logic according to Kantian Principles*, p. 47, §. 106, to say nothing of others).

*Rem.* Through *Kantian* principles the exposure given would have been happily masked. How so?—Answer: because their need of a quite *Transcendental* Logic, which has [p. 14] a manifold of sensibility already *a priori* lying before it, (convenient!) *Critique of Pure Reason*, 2nd ed., p. 102,—postulated again the renewal of the distinction between *finite* and *infinite* judgments (ibid., p. 97); hence thereby sanctioned anew the relation of negation *either to the copula in negative judgments, or to the predicate in infinite* affirmative judgments.

c) Every copula (No. 1–4) expresses a *modus specialis* of thinking itself; hence the *categorical* copula too is the *formal* (what is thought) of a categorical judgment; subject and predicate are, as concepts, as one has hitherto understood it, the *material*. (Let one judge accordingly how *pure* our hitherto *Pure* Logics were). Limitation of what was said under No. c, according to results the citation of which does not yet belong here.

### *Einschränkung des, unter Num. c gesagten, nach Resultaten, deren Anführung noch nicht hieher gehört.*

It remains the case that every copula (1–4) expresses a *modus specialis* of thinking itself. Yet the first two among them, the *categorical* (A in A), and the *consequence* [p. 15] (A *through* A) belong to thinking as thinking, without application of the A to B and Non-B of a cognition, (they are *pure modi*). The last two, on the other hand, are repetitions of the first two on the *two-part* nature of an object in cognition, a nature that does not alter *thinking as thinking* through its duplicity, as B and Non-B.

*Rem.* I note that with this last I have already said something there which perhaps I should not yet have said. But it is difficult to avoid it, and, by touching on it, nonetheless not to disturb the intelligibility of one’s exposition, so long as one cannot yet explain the same. Let one therefore lay this B and Non-B aside each time where its summoning in the present context is unavoidable, and hold out, *as long as it must be*, by the eternal stream of the A as A, repeatable infinitely many times *in A as through A*, until it will of itself plunge into life over the *two-part* cataract of B and Non-B.—I must distract myself,—and am coming upon the solution of a riddle for lovers of a speculative pastime. For the question here again returns quite naturally: how it may have come about, 1) that one first saw in the [p. 16] *Ergo* that it cannot be negated? To this the answer has already been given: because most matter *attaches* to it *in thinking*; (so I must still express myself now, until it can be put into German); 2) how it may have come about that one then also saw this in the disjunction in disjunctive judgments? Answer, because the disjunction too, like the syllogism, still belongs to the *mixed* modi of thinking: *Kantians*; 3) how it may have come about that one finally saw this also in the *one pure* modus of thinking? here I have nothing to do but to name the name *Maaß*; although even by him the consequence could not yet be thought in its necessary purity (without hypothesis). 4) Whether it then occurred to nobody to make the logicians a reproach on account of their illogical representations of the other *pure* modus of thinking, of the *formal* of a categorical judgment? Answer: *Kant* made them a reproach about it (*Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 140, 141). But it went with him as it would always have gone with an opposition to the *Ergo*. He wonders, accuses them of a fault, but does not help, but makes the evil worse, and had to, *by virtue of the* [p. 17] *basic design of his Critique*, not only make the evil worse, but make the harm incurable for the orthodox. How so?—Because he, *by virtue of the basic design of his Critique*, leads man through categories first to pure reason, that is, *lets him first cognize and judge, and only then think*.

### Brief Condensation of the Result from §. 12.

If one understands by *logical*, as has hitherto happened in all logics and in the *pure* ones in particular, the *formal* of *thinking itself*, our *work of reason* (= A) in every concept: then there is absolutely no logical negation except contradiction, i.e. non-thinking. The Non might come from wherever it liked: in thinking, as *thinking*, no difference of quality, hence no Non, no *Yes or No* can take place.

### Elucidation.

An *Imperative*,—and this is the *whole* (not only the half practical), *comprehended under the law of identity, thinking* (A be eternally = A), can never become a non-imperative, however it may command or forbid with regard to an object [p. 18] This one surely recognizes; but did one also think of it already? And has one learned the imperative of the *One-times-One*?—

### Application of What Has Been Said to All Logics, and to the Kantian Critique *in specie*.

*Logical* means, then, according to all logics, *the formal of thinking itself*, and yet, according to all logics, a logical reality (hence a thought) is supposed to be capable of being canceled only through its (surely also logical) negation (through a non-thought), *but nevertheless still remain a thought*?—Certainly; for the *Critique*, the Sun, as a reviewer expressed himself, for the philosophy of all centuries, has indeed even discovered a *form of thinking* (category) of *negation*, in order to refresh itself for centuries also on this. Much good may it do everyone in need of light who can think through negations and limitations, as his forms of thinking. This is what comes out of it when one holds the law of identity (hence A or thinking in every judgment)—itself for a judgment; why not then altogether for a *synthetic* one?

[p. 19] Provided with the knot of apriority, it would then hold as firmly as an *analytic* one.

### Incidentally a few words on the discovery of the Critique under the name of an *a priori* synthesis; the rest follows later.

Whoever assumes synthetic judgments *a priori* already puts judgments into thinking, as thinking *itself*. What is to be thought of this, our German word *Urtheil* [judgment] (*quasi divisio primaria Objecti* [as it were, the primary division of the object]) may provisionally announce.

Whoever assumes synthetic judgments *a priori*, assumes, besides the law of identity, which he does not give up (*tacite*) recognizing as analytic and placing before all thinking, although already as a judgment, (for he cannot) still something non-analytic in thinking as thinking, he assumes, I say, still something non-analytic, which now either must nevertheless first be posited under the analysis of thinking (A repeatable infinitely many times as A), hence is in itself not yet a thinking, or which, just as it grew together synthetically of itself, also independently of A (as a B, C, D, E, etc.) has its analysis *in itself*; consequently he assumes [p. 20] a thinking as a B-thinking, and then again a thinking as a C-thinking, and so on through the whole synthetic alphabet.—What sort of new postulates these are, the law of contradiction reminds us.

Whoever proceeds thus with the law of identity cuts off the head of philosophy (from Critical desperation, as *Reinhold* through his arbitrariness?) in order then, with an original, droll, creative, i.e. *exclusively Kantian* wit (hence also *only to him alone* is the business well suited), in place of the severed head, taken up again merely by the way, to be able to make a multitude of *a priori* knots, each of which has only to promote *one* piece of the trunk to the high unity with itself (i.e. properly only with him who makes and imitates the knots), and which therefore let the whole be frayed into a highly manifold of needless little pieces, and consequently recommend themselves uncommonly to the lecterns, etc., as a surrogate for the flogged-out metaphysics.—The *Königsberg man* knows his nation; for he is an excellent *empiricist*, hence a brilliant *judger* (knows how to turn a style onto everything, as one might put that synthesis-work among us)—But what shall one call you then, *Leibniz*, [p. 21] *Wolff, Bilfinger, Baumgarten, Mendelssohn, Eberhard, Schwab, Maaß* etc.?—Only analysts, because you are no synthesists in the *a priori* genus? No, *thinkers*. The rest follows later.

### Test of the 12th §.

Place two zeros together without a preceding One, and they signify nothing. Let one hear one of the great nation of the new Romans pronounce his Non, Non *separated*, and he merely *doubles* thereby his *negation*. But if Non Non is combined in a thought *as thought*, e.g. in a predicate *as thought* (not as concept), hence is to be drawn to the copula of a concept or to the categorical copula (No. 1 and 5): then the *grammarians* remark, it *doubles its affirmation*. It posits at least. Odd; just too common for a *Faculty*. Even as long as the Transcendental spool still ran empty, one had too much to do in the important articles of conversion and contraposition of a proposition etc., for one to have been able to occupy oneself, in a manner befitting a faculty, with such trifles in a philosophical scruple, perhaps obtained from *grammarians*. Without [p. 22] knowing, like *Wieland’s* chancellor, an answer to every question, it might also, upon actually effected condescension to a *Rescribatur*, have been hard to resolve the *Dubia Grammaticorum* [doubts of the grammarians], after the copula had once been denied, and through the Critique the negating had been raised altogether to a form of thinking of its own. *Scribatur itaque Logicis:* [Let it therefore be written to the logicians:]

*An attempt made on thinking itself, to duplicate it, after the manner of what is represented in thinking, also in its own quality, posits again simply what had previously been posited, hence does not duplicate; but only makes (in a judgment) the test of this, that A repeatable infinitely many times as A, without giving up thinking itself, cannot become, just so, also Non A, as B in representation certainly can also become Non B*; (in what sense this latter, see below!)

The next test of this, hence of the law of identity, is the proposition of contradiction itself, in which thinking quite properly assigns itself its own essence (A repeatable infinitely many times [p. 23] as A) as the object of a judgment, which *must* then turn out thus:

A repeatable infinitely many times as A, excludes all Non-A in the repetition; (Logical negation, if by *logical*, as hitherto, the formal of thinking itself is understood, is a contradiction)

In all propositions now which read like *B non est non-B* [B is not non-B], or *Nullus doctus est non-doctus* [No learned man is unlearned], one makes *on any arbitrary object* the same test, that an A once posited in B can, by no attempt at a negation carried over from the object B also onto A (the thought), duplicate itself in its quality, or in any way alter itself at all.

### Conclusion of the 12th §.

Should one suppose that *logicians* would have been able to infer the law of identity first from the proposition of contradiction, hence to have tried the *excluding* beforehand, before anything was yet posited? They could; for they did it—see all the newest logics. The ground?—because the Sun, *with a merely negative criterion of all truth*, that is, with an eclipse of the sun, had risen. [p. 24] See the *ipsissima verba* [very words] of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 190.

### §. 13.

*Thinking, as thinking, however, also tolerates no difference of quantity; but is always A, repeatable infinitely many times as A in all possible cases of its use, hence Universal in the strictest sense with exclusion of the possibility of the opposite.*

### Observation.

This is evident also from the fact that no thought *as thought* admits a difference of quantity. Take the *Ergo*.

### Corollaries.

1) Since an *Omne* [all] very well allows the opposite *of itself*, *as an Omne*, to be represented in a *Nullus* [none], *Quidam* [some] and *Hic* [this]: the positing of thinking in an *Omne*-thinking would, just as well as the positing of the same in a particular- or singular-thinking, make thinking itself into a non-thinking.

2) What is actually thought in the concept *hic homo* [this man] must just as well be infinitely [p. 25] many times repeatable in all possible cases of its use, as what is actually thought in the concept *quidam homines* [some men], or *omnes homines* [all men].

*Rem.* It has already occurred to me several times that one might take offense at the expression: *in all possible cases of its use*. What is that supposed to mean: *Possible* here? a Critic will object. But let one not be angry too soon; it would be in vain: for, why might I not say it?—*I am already looking ahead.*

3) The *Omnes, Quidam, Hic*, accordingly cannot at all be sought in *thinking, as thinking*, as a designation of a difference of quantity. It cannot be a modus of *thinking, as thinking*, like the copula in the *categorical* judgment, and in the consequence.

4) In *thinking, as thinking*, *Omnes, Quidam, Hic*, are rather perfectly one.—One can even, on the very same object, according as circumstances command, have to apply the same thinking in an *Omne, Quoddam* or *Hoc*. The limitation or extension thereby is not possible through an alteration [p. 26] of *thinking, as thinking*; this would be a no-more-thinking, hence a contradiction. It can therefore also not be determined in thinking already through thinking, as the modi of the copula are. It cannot be determined in thinking through the object, *as object*; *object, as object*, has itself no difference of quantity. It must accordingly be a mere repetition of the same thinking (together with the essence of its repeatability infinitely many times as A) *according to given rows of association in the imagination* (not phantasy), which now demands the one time a *Quidam*, as sign of its present aggregate in the imagination, then an *Omnes*, as a sign to the same end, then a *Hic*. Here I had to take refuge for the first time in our *imagining-into the world* (*tanquam informationes nostras in mundum* [as it were, our formations into the world]) in order to provide the thinking of my readers themselves with a footing, necessary here, and in the sequel nonetheless not harmful to the pure resumption of the A, as A.

5) In *thinking, as thinking*, not only *universal and singular*, as one has hitherto assumed, but also all *particular* categorical judgments are perfectly equal to one another; for in thinking, as *thinking*, they can have no [p. 27] difference of quantity; this difference might come from wherever it liked: it can lie neither in thinking, nor in the representation of an object, as object; since the latter brings with it indeed the possibility of a *difference of quality*, but by no means a *difference of quantity* (as object) into its representability (in thinking).

*Rem.* From this it follows what is to be thought of it, when one in the hitherto logics (*Jakob*, 3rd ed., pp. 52, 53) applied the difference of quantity even so far as to the *extent of concepts, as concepts*, (without their being yet already combined in a judgment), and divided them in this respect into *universal and particular*, finally, with the proper direction as to what is illogical about it, even further into *singular* ones (individual, ib. §. 119.). *Kiesewetter* suspected nothing good in this quantity-taxation of an object, as object, or even of a thinking as thinking; for otherwise I would not know what could be distinguished *logically* (consequently in the *thought-work proper*, as one has hitherto understood the logical) in a concept; (*Compendium of a General Logic*, pp. 22, 23). He says therefore first only: *“formerly one divided the* [p. 28] *concepts according to their extent into universal, particular and singular,”* protests then against the division, but instead of pursuing this hint further, he finally springs from the matter which could here alone be in question; whether namely a *concept, considered in and for itself and outside a judgment*, admits a difference of quantity, suddenly off, and discovers that one could after all call the one concept a *universal* one, the other a *particular* one, if one wished to compare them with each other in respect of their spheres, that is, if one held their spheres against each other *in a judgment*, consequently ceased to consider each of them as *a concept in and for itself*.—Hit; for that was precisely the subject; was it not? No, for I leaf through a few pages, and find, p. 34, in *Kiesewetter* himself, that this properly would first have belonged in his doctrine of the relation of concepts among themselves, hence not to the quantity of a concept, *as concept*; find also, that there *universal* and *particular* concepts are from then on no longer mentioned, but the expression *higher* and *lower* concepts is, as customary, again [p. 29] put in its place. With this we would be, then, again where we were, namely at the question: is there then no difference of quantity in a concept, *in and for itself, and outside a judgment, considered*, *and in how far is there none*? There is none in so far as there can be none, thus 1) *in so far as every concept must be a thought*, 2) *in so far as in every concept an object as object* must be represented, an object as object, however, admits no difference of quantity. What therefore in a concept *considered in and for itself* (perhaps for some heads not sufficiently capable of abstraction) might still appear to be a *logical* difference of quantity, of that one may be sure that it belongs neither to the work of reason nor to the representation of an object as object in this concept, but absolutely to its *materiature*. This materiature (the regard to the work of sense) is after all, according to all logics, something unlogical; consequently, according to all logics, their own division of concepts into universal, particular, singular, is unlogical; and if it is so for concepts, so it is also for judgments; the latter goes without saying, as it seems to me, since *par ratio* of No. 1, 2, in the case [p. 30] of judgments, as in that of concepts, applies.—Why, however, did *Jakob* want to reprove something of the kind only in the *singular* concepts? Why does *Kiesewetter* make that which one commonly passes off as singular concepts, e.g. the *nomina propria*, outright mere *signs* for the representations of single objects (intuitions)? p. 22, l. c. This compels me myself to go into the singular.—*With signs for the representations* we thus began our representations in *Kiesewetter*; for we surely begin with the singular, and not with the universal, in our representations?—With something unlogical or half-logical we began in *Jakob*; for an individual concept belongs indeed logically to the universal ones (as every singular judgment belongs logically to the universal ones), so it stands everywhere, (only not in *Kiesewetter*, perhaps through a misprint? p. 56), written; but there clings to it, *as individual*, also something unlogical, namely a peculiar mark of a single object or of an individual, so stands written in *Jakob* l. c.——I do not know, am I blind, or is it, here as there, still never day?—How would it be, then, if we [p. 31] human beings (not we logicians), were for once to let us human beings begin with thinking, the categories of *totality* etc. *hactenus* in honor?—There is, then, once a thinking = A there, and now, for all I care, a sensible material = C is to be there too. This materiature (= C) must, *as materiature*, be annihilated by thinking in thinking, otherwise no thought comes of it. A *nomen proprium* is a thought, like a *nomen nonproprium*; consequently it must, by the same process as every concept, be made into a *thought*, that is, not merely into a *sign for representations* (in which thinking in thinking ran out on *Kiesewetter*, after the latest German fashion), but, as said, into a true, perfect thought, for which the *actual* cases of its use are now determined by that intuition, which preceded it. This is the pervasive process in everything that is to be represented in a (human) thinking!—There is, then, no universal, no particular and no singular thought- and object-being. What is thought is thought; object is object. But I said: the actual cases of its use were, for that *nomen proprium*, [p. 32] determined by the intuition which preceded it. How? if now with us human beings, according to our generic kind of world-imagination, *in one* intuition several intuitions could already be determined, depending on whether something from outside, the necessary good occasion, were to come to it? More briefly, and perhaps more clearly, how? if we human beings, according to our generic kind of world-imagination, with an intuition once had, on occasion understood *multiplying*? Then it might be that in such cases, where the multiplication is permitted by a material at hand, the materiature, as materiature, would be annihilated by thinking in thinking *just once*, and in such cases it would be annihilated once and for all. This I would let myself call a *concept*; but such a concept would then be for A what a *nomen proprium* is for the same, and a *nomen proprium* would be for A what a concept is for the same. So *Jakob* must surely have meant, when, according to logical custom, and, as I therefore believe, without misprint, he said l. c. *that an individual concept belongs among the universal ones.* But if *Jakob* then takes offense at the peculiar mark of the individual concept, [p. 33] as something unlogical, nonetheless: then it could never occur to him, by virtue of all previous logics, and by virtue of the Critique least of all, that a concept, a whole complexion of truly sensible, and hence also sensibly remaining marks, is consequently *logically*, i.e. as was hitherto meant, *for thinking itself*, wholly one with an intuition (better: *apprehension*).—The length of the row in which A can be repeated at once makes no difference to the matter. If one wishes to push it still further, one can say that the concepts that are singular (not logically, as one hitherto understood it, but) *according to their materiature*, e.g. the famous *nomina propria Kant* and *Kiesewetter*, are purer thoughts than the universal concept *man* (*genus humanum*), *purer are they*, not through a contradiction with my A eternally equal to A; but because less materiature may be annihilated by thinking in thinking in their representation than in a representation of the whole human race in thinking. The concepts that are particular (not logically, as it was always understood, but) *according to their materiature* are either one carried out with the (world-imagination) sensible complexion of a certain [p. 34] All in the representation—a *division*:

*Omnes homines sunt mortales* [All men are mortal] *Quidam homines sunt mortales* [Some men are mortal]

or they are a *subtraction* carried out on the sensible complexion of a certain All in the representation:

*Omnes homines sunt homines* [All men are men] *Quidam homines sunt docti homines.* [Some men are learned men.]

Since now a *hic* homo (taken exclusively) too is a subtraction from an All in the representation: I cannot take it amiss in *Kiesewetter* if, even without misprint, merely weary of the old unintelligible proposition, on which *Jakob* still hung: *propositiones singulares aequipollent universalibus* [singular propositions are equipollent to universal ones]; he counted the *singular* judgments among the particular ones. He meant precisely the subtraction.—The further course will give, as I hope, satisfactory elucidation of everything that is asserted in this Remark. It cannot fail, so long as I do not become inconsistent; and only the misunderstanding of some expressions brought in here by way of elucidation, such as that of multiplication etc., could *up to now* draw upon me the appearance of an incorrect- [p. 35] ness in my assertions, which, however, under the stated presupposition, *must* disappear again. To those who should not be able to find their way into the annihilation of *materiature as materiature* in thinking *as thinking*, because under the 12th § I let the syllogism, e.g., connect the most matter in thinking (this meant precisely: *annihilate as matter*), I give it to *consider* what they may then have represented to themselves hitherto under *form* as opposed to *material*, if it was not a matter annihilated in thinking *as thinking*? I request them further to tell me whether their eye would be able to see, their ear to hear, if the former did not annihilate the rays of light and the air, the latter the air, *as objects* in a seeing or hearing, through the act of seeing or hearing itself; whether they could subsist before the tumult of the little organic world in them, as that which they are, as *these human beings*, if their materiature, the circulation of their juices, their business of digestion and secretion, the thousand piece-works (little fibers etc. with their labor) of which their physical man is composed, were not annihilated *in their representation*? I take the liberty of asking them yet [p. 36] one more thing, which of course, as a weak copy, can no longer speak so strongly for my cause as the original. It is: whether any artist, or indeed craftsman, would be able to *form* anything, if he did not, over the existence of what is to be formed (notwithstanding that this continues forever *in rerum natura* [in the nature of things]) draw over it another existence, spread it, or by means of fire (as in meltings) pour it out, and hence annihilate the former in this respect? Finally, if to anyone a *concept*, as a determinate kind of our human world-imagination, as a mere complexion of truly sensible, and hence also sensibly remaining marks (merely the A *so far* deducted from them), in which the human child begins his career through the act of a multiplication carried out first on *one* image (e.g. of the mother), if all this should by no means be evident, him I can perhaps meanwhile soothe by the reminder that through this odd assertion he can, so to speak as through a hypothesis, explain the still far more wondrous phenomenon: why philosophers so gladly become poets, whereas this cannot befall mathematicians *in mathematics* in the same way; explain [p. 37] consequently, how it may also have come about that from the previous *metaphysical ligata* [verse], even in a Critique of Pure Reason, merely a *transcendental ligata* (called *synthesis a priori*) could grow. A burnt-out poet, e.g., now goes over to us *absque saltu* [without a leap] to the categories, and finds roof and shelter there again, as if he were at home. One wishes, namely, to remark (§. 2, Rem.) that according to this Critique, in which the categories are recorded, thinking may only come afterwards, and that therefore in it (as can only now be added) no pure A is any longer to be had, but an A mixed with B and Non B, even with C, consequently an *uncommonly manifold*, entirely new thing, on which a poet to whom it ran out *a posteriori* can now try out *a priori* much that is pretty.

6) *In thinking, as thinking, neither hypothetical nor disjunctive judgments have a difference of quantity.*

*Rem. Maaß* saw it in them. It was for the shrewd man as if this must be so. But he did not know why, and therefore substituted here too a *falsum medium*.

[p. 38] I owe him an account of my assertion. Here it is.—He says (p. 143, l. c.) “quality and quantity do not belong to the hypothetical judgment, *as such*, (and he says this p. 154 also of the disjunctive, appealing to the very same ground); this is evident from the fact that the said quality and quantity also occur in the antecedent and the consequent when these are not at all connected into a hypothetical judgment, but are considered quite singly for themselves.” So runs his *medius*. Analyzed, it rests on the unobjectionable remark that what is already there cannot first be brought along by one who is only now arriving; but now quality and quantity are already there in the categorical antecedent and consequent, considered singly, of which a hypothetical judgment consists: therefore a hypothetical judgment, as *hypothetical*, cannot first bring quantity and quality with it.—But how does it follow from this that it is now itself to have no quality and quantity at all? Do not the categorical judgments, of which it consists, bring both along? Answer: “1) as *hypothetical* it has none, 2) the quality and quantity brought along come into consideration here (in a [p. 39] hypothetical judgment, as according to p. 154 in a disjunctive one) only *as matter*, no longer as form, i.e. it is, as form, by means of the hypothesis, now annihilated in thinking by thinking.” How? can that, however, which is form, *as form*, ever become matter, notwithstanding that what is matter, *as matter*, is annihilated by the form? Can this be, can thinking, through a thinking, annihilate itself, as a thinking or having-thought, (for this is one in thinking) without contradiction? And if now this cannot be, is it possible that the quality and quantity, now annihilated, could previously have belonged to the *form* of a categorical judgment?—The Maaßian *medius* would then run thus: “because the *previous form* of a categorical judgment, and with it also its quality and quantity, is *now*, in its passage into a hypothetical one, through the *hypothetical* of the latter (likewise in the disjunction), annihilated into *matter*, hence *as form*: so the hypothetical judgment, *as such* (likewise the disjunctive), has neither quality nor quantity any more.” This, however, would be no *falsus medius*—annihilation of form, *as form*, would be thinkable? Further!

[p. 40] *Maaß* says, moreover: “because hypothetical (likewise disjunctive) judgments no longer have subject and predicate: so by virtue of the *former* the *quality* ceases in them.” Now, however, in a hypothetical judgment (likewise in a disjunctive) subject and predicate are nonetheless present as parts of categorical judgments, hence as concepts; consequently they are now, since they cease, according to *Maaß*, to be subject and predicate, present *as matter*. Had they, however, as subject and predicate in a categorical judgment ever belonged to the *form* of a categorical judgment: then the same case would now occur with them as with quality and quantity, that now, through their use as a matter, thinking, through a thinking, annihilated itself as a thinking or having-thought (for this is one in thinking). In the same way it stands with that which in the little word *Est* in a categorical judgment belongs to the form. If it is form: then it remains form, and can, whatever one may begin with the categorical judgment, *as form*, never become matter in another judgment, i.e. never be annihilated without contradiction.—Put together, the *Maaßian* [p. 41] *medius* runs: “because 1) *quality and quantity* 2) *subject and predicate* of a categorical judgment become matter in a hypothetical (and disjunctive) judgment: so the hypothetical (and disjunctive) judgment has no difference of quality or quantity.” According to this he assumes either that the form can pass over into matter, that thinking can be annihilated by a thinking; or he assumes that quality and quantity, subject and predicate in a categorical judgment, *never* belonged *to form*, i.e. never to thinking, *as thinking*. In the former case he assumes a contradiction, in the other he would contradict his own principles on the categorical judgment, p. 90, 91, etc.; for otherwise he would, as is self-evident, already in the year 1793 have had to be entirely of my opinion. This, however, he was only in the thesis of No. 6, that neither hypothetical nor disjunctive judgments *can* have a difference of quantity (and, as was shown before) also none of quality; by no means in their foundations, without which it nonetheless falls. The *Kantians* simply give to hypothetical and disjunctive judgments the quantity of universality, as *of an Omne*,—

[p. 42] (*Omne*—Then, *Omne*—Either-or); because no exception takes place there?—No; rather because no *Omne* takes place there (if judgment is ever to be passed inductively), hypothetical and disjunctive judgments have no quantity at all.—I return to *Maaß*. How does it come about that *Maaß* was *able* to set up an entirely correct thesis, and yet this very correct thesis from a *falsus medius*? How could he, *without a well-advised object for an Ergo* (permit me meanwhile so to paraphrase the *medius*) nonetheless bring out a true proposition in the conclusion? Answer: it was not *his* Ergo that brought out this true proposition for him; he had quite properly ceased to connect his representations in a thinking (for the *ceasing to think*, as I would still remind once more, is always to be understood so), when he came *to this* Ergo in his representation; had consequently only written down an Ergo for himself in his representation, where nothing of his representations any longer was connected in thinking, hence for thinking a Non-Ergo, i.e. a not-having-been-thought, a contradiction, was there. *But the thesis was already before, and from a prius qualified for inferring,* [p. 43] *correct*. As something that *must* be correct, it struck the keen sense of the man when he observed the hypothetical and disjunctive judgments; for keen sense is, taken as *sense*, the sprightliest forerunner of the *Why*. But since he now consequently looked about for a *medius only now*: he hit, for lack of any previous analysis of thinking *as thinking*, merely upon an *observation* which could be made on all such judgments. The observation was again correct, and went through for all inductively (I say *inductively*). On *all* of them it could indeed be shown 1) that already the categorical judgments, of which they consist, *considered singly*, have quality, quantity, predicate and subject, that consequently the hypothetical and disjunctive judgments do not first bring this with them; on *all* of them it showed itself 2) in the *observation*, that the supervening hypothesis or disjunction consumes the predicate and subject, the quality and quantity, *as that which they were before in a categorical judgment*, now in thinking. The business seemed correct; it was *shown* (monstrated, but not *demonstrated*). But now it was surely also to be rendered an account of [p. 44] the correct observation made. This observation had given quite unambiguously to be recognized a being-crushed of the predicate and subject, of the quality and quantity of categorical judgments through the thinking of the same in hypothetical and disjunctive ones. The account had therefore to turn out thus: “because predicate and subject, quality and quantity of the categorical judgments are so evidently crushed in the thinking of the same under a hypothesis or disjunction: so the only thing to blame for the quality- and quantity-lessness of the hypothetical and disjunctive judgments must be *that the named pieces of the categorical judgments become matter in those*.” And yet, according to all logics, subject and predicate, quality and quantity belonged to the *form* of categorical judgments; for otherwise these logics would not have been *pure* in all these pieces? And yet no form as form can be made matter, no thinking crushed by thinking in thinking? What was then the Ergo *in the representation*? A Non-ergo in *thinking*, i.e. a contradiction.—Herewith I believe I have described the course of things in all previous logics. Most of their theses were cor- [p. 45] rect, and as theses without fault; for what they asserted could be shown inductively. *But all rested on errors, which through the Kantian Critique became altogether unforeseeable.* If what is to happen happens: then what has happened, *as having happened*, is no fault; one can show the performance *as it ought to be*, to which *Kant* too appeals at once in his Preface to the *Critique of P. R.* But it can nonetheless be a *gross error* on that account. What has happened need only have arisen from a *supposed* Why, which was merely a Why *in the representation*. Error is a *deficit* in the use of thinking itself, and since from thinking *as thinking* no fault can proceed, a ceasing of the connecting of certain representations in a thinking, a not-thinking of certain marks, coalesced in the connection of a *mere* association; in short every error is a contradiction, be what has happened *as having happened* as it ought to be, or not as it ought to be, the error itself be contradiction through *inconsistency*, whereby, after repeated positings of A in B, finally a Non-A occurs in the series, or sheer contradiction at A and [p. 46] Non-A in one breath, like the old logical witticism of a Sidiroxylon. All theses of our previous logics, however correct they might be as theses, rested on errors, hence on contradictions. This is evident already from their presumption of a pure formal thought-work in them, since they nevertheless worked in the material; the further process in this one may see in the above thesis. One good head after another, namely, found such theses acutely, proceeded inductively with them, tried them on examples, found in these the *probatum est* of the thesis found, which he had already divined, indisputable, and therefore believed he had nothing but form, nothing but work of pure thinking in them. But when an account of the correctness was to be rendered: then he had to annihilate that which he had previously assumed as the form of concepts, in judgments, and his supposed form of judgments, in syllogisms, into sheer matter in thinking by thinking, consequently, had he reflected upon it, to declare himself guilty of a series of errors or contradictions in his logic; as indeed it was really at this odd annihilation of form that my own reflection on the ground or groundlessness of all our previous logics awoke.

[p. 47] 7) *In thinking, as thinking, no syllogism, be it categorical, hypothetical or disjunctive, has a difference of quantity.* (A syllogism is A, expressed in the copula *Therefore*; and if A connects so much matter at once, consequently annihilates it *qua* matter, as it can connect at once in man, consequently annihilate *qua* matter: then it is expressed through *Therefore*).

*Rem.* Here first, with the syllogisms, *Kant* broke through. He succeeded, there where it annihilates the most matter as matter in thinking, in approaching nearer to the essence of thinking than had happened before him. Reason, or thinking *as thinking*, goes upon something *unconditioned*, in that it, *as thinking*, always is what it is, yet also, *as mere thinking*, has no thing already nor can have. Herein he would have been right and seen further herein than his predecessors, provided that he would have recognized how with his *unconditioned*, in the indicated sense, he was really just beginning, happily, for once, to describe merely the process of thinking itself in the *Est*, e.g., in short in A with its repeatability infinitely many times. *The unconditioned is a thing,* [p. 48] *or yields a* thing (whatever it may be). Herein he was mightily wrong. It stands with this as follows. What in the *unconditioned*, which *Kant* sets as the goal of reason only in *syllogizing*, is *actual* reason (the rest does not concern us here), that is, as said, nothing other than thinking itself *in its complete quality- and quantity-lessness*, hence, considered from this point of view too, a *true* (only not afterwards again *Kantianly* twisted) but true *unconditioned*, which cannot even posit an object (without a preceding C) merely out of itself and through itself; that therefore (*in itself and through itself*) posits nothing but itself. But instead of descending into the depth which began to open before him, he took from himself and from all inquirers who stepped in his footsteps every opportunity ever again to cast a sidelong glance into it earlier than only at *syllogizing*. Indeed, even through the disclosure about itself which it seemed to confide to this peculiar man, the business of thinking was, in a new hasty *will-to-know*, thoroughly and irretrievably confused. The *will-to-know* was, moreover, this time set on something strange, [p. 49] set merely on *knowing for certain that in metaphysics* (*as Kant meant it with her*) *one knows nothing*. Socratic; but did one need to go to such lengths for that?—Only *Kant’s* genius could, through so many eloquent pages (from p. 349 on, l. c.), hide from himself and others in the most artful way the contradictions in which he, his attention fixed undividedly on that war of extermination, gigantically bustled about. That they are contradictions is now briefly to be proved. In the first place, it seems to one that it already stands badly with an assertion if, of the hypothesis on which it rests, the exact contrary can be assumed, and the given problem can now be solved through this assumed contrary just as correctly or incorrectly as it happened through that hypothesis itself, consequently just as satisfactorily. Had *Maaß* seen in the actual thought-work in *categorical* judgments, too, its complete quality- and quantity-lessness, just as shrewdly as he saw it in the hypothetical and disjunctive judgments: then he must have been able to bring out from the former the (though according to *Kant* merely dialectical) unavoidability of a transcendental doctrine of the soul, from the hypothetical the (though [p. 50] according to *Kant* merely dialectical) unavoidability of a transcendental doctrine of the world, from the disjunctive the (though according to *Kant* merely dialectical) unavoidability of a doctrine of God, to a hair, as *Kant* himself brought all this out of the form of the categorical, hypothetical and disjunctive *syllogisms of reason*. In this way, namely, everything in the judgments would already have been so prepared that the unconditioned, that is, precisely *the mere thinking itself* in the *Est* in categorical judgments, would apparently have had to be able to ground also an unconditioned *subject* for our appearances of mind, hence a *soul*, the unconditioned in the *If* (or rather in the *Then*) an unconditioned subject for the outer appearances, hence a *world-whole*, the unconditioned in the *Either-or* an unconditioned subject for everything together, hence the *idea of a God*, entirely in the *Kantian* manner. But not from the judgments, but merely from the nature, opposed by him to the judgments in this respect, of the *pure syllogisms of reason*, which are supposed to press exclusively upon an unconditioned (i.e. upon thinking?), did *Kant* indeed want to derive those metaphysical ideas. The foundation can thus not [p. 51] possibly hold, since from his assumed contrary the same inferences for amateurs could be brought about.—But a striving after the unconditioned, which, as this striving after an unconditioned, posits a *thing*; a syllogism which (according to *Kant* himself) is a mere pure thinking, and yet, as this mere pure thinking, produces objects: a thing in itself produced in this way, which yet is no thing in itself, but an idea; three things in themselves, which are no things in themselves, but merely three ideas: three ideas wherein God, soul, world, as perfectly parallel to one another? flow forth from one and the same essence of thinking without further ado; a categorical syllogism, which could not be more categorical than in the idea of a God, and which yet forces on me only my own soul (dialectically): a hypothetical syllogism, which could not be more hypothetical than in the usual metaphysical idea of my soul, and which yet forces on me the world as a whole (dialectically); a disjunctive syllogism, which could be in nothing more disjunctive than in my human world-views, and which yet forces on me the idea of a non-world-being, of a God (dialectically): a pure reason that [p. 52] there, where it becomes purest, *merely by the very fact that it becomes purest, must* deceive; a thinking, then, which, there where it shows itself and can show itself most in its peculiar essence, is pleased to perform for me a dialectical juggling-show in three acts (not counting the scenes), mind you *merely out of itself as this thinking with its peculiar essence*, and hence, doubtless, without degrading itself thereby to a no-longer-thinking: a synthesis, consequently a judgment, one knows not whence flown? as condition of the possibility of the purest thinking; a syllogism, as condition of the possibility of the purest inferring, yet at the same time a false syllogism, and the purest inferring a false inferring:—these are rarities without equal, at which pure reason itself might well stand still. But with this unceasing skirmishing around thinking, yet only ever getting farther downward from the right spot, that humbles speculation once more, and humbles it,—who should doubt it?—in its boldest advocate.—*Maaß* was in his assertion of the complete lack of quality and quantity in the hypothetical and disjunctive judgments yet, within human memory, [p. 53] for the first time (even still outside the doctrine of syllogisms) advanced beyond the barrier of totality (*Omnitudo*), without suspecting how much he had broken through thereby. *Kant*, far from lighting, in his new doctrine of syllogisms, upon the *Ergo*, as the sole thing devoid of quality and quantity, hence the properly *unconditioned* in the syllogism (as a work of thought), and thereby paving for himself the *only right* way beyond the *Omnitudo*, proceeds instead from the universality of the *predicate* in the major premise, which, as he says (l. c. p. 379), we have thought in the major premise in its whole extent *under a certain condition*. “*This completed magnitude of the extent*, he adds, *in relation to such a condition*, is called *universality* (*Universalitas*).” Close to the right goal—compare his excellent explanation, peculiar to him, of the *absolute*, to which he ascends, from the universality of the predicate in the major premise, *hence as a splendid empiricist*, finally, from p. 382–385; but the *prius* for it was not found, but rather incorrectly stated, and consequently everything at once took a wrong direction again. It was dawning strongly, [p. 54] that one sees: (hence perhaps the loud outcry); day did not break; of this whoever can read and think may convince himself, if he will read further in the cited text; (hence perhaps the *motus retrogradus* of certain planets around the sun,—were it possible, wholly toward midnight).—A thinking, e.g., which, *as a thinking*, pressed for *totality*, i.e. for the ending of itself *as a thinking*, and whereby *Kant* precisely at once distorts again his so promisingly stirred-up thought of the absolute (l. c.), would be no thinking any more, but, considered *as a thinking* (as a beginning of pure reason), is a contradiction. Enough, then!

## Test on the 13th §.

First a request. Do reflect, then, why it is one and the same in a geometrical figure, I will suppose, in the calculation of an isosceles triangle, whether one says: *hoc triangulum aequicrurum* [this isosceles triangle], or *quoddam triangulum aequicrurum* [a certain isosceles triangle], or *omne triangulum aequicrurum* [every isosceles triangle]. In *one* Δ *aequicruro*, *some aequicrura* are calculated, and *all aequicrura* are calculated. Should, with actual quantities, the hitherto so-called [p. 55] *logical quantitas*, as it seems, become superfluous? Were *pure* (blind) intuitions not the fashion now, I should almost believe that this must come merely from the essence of thinking itself and perhaps also from the nature of an object *as object*. However, even this will show itself in the proper place. Meanwhile the representation of a something as a something, of an object as object, may take the place of that Δ; for already with this, *one* is like *all*, and *all* like *some*. Now to the test!

Not an *Omne* negated to the *Omne*, but a *Quoddam* negated to the *Omne*, gives exclusively a *contradiction*; so runs the assertion of the logics in the doctrine of *judgments*. But if one now finds in any logic a satisfactory account of this, or even only a compatibility of what was taught of the principle of contradiction with what was taught of contradicting judgments; then I willingly take back all my previous assertions, supposing that they could be taken back. *Omne* and *Non-Omne* are supposed, namely, merely for this reason to be not *contradicting* (*contradictorium*) but only *conflicting* (*contrarium*), *because already the Non-Quoddam under* [p. 56] *an Omne contains a contradiction*? A conflict is supposed to be that, when something positive is still added to the contradiction in concepts; thus a plus to a contradiction, and yet no contradiction any more, but a mere conflict? (See *Maaß* p. 30 and all *Kantian* logics). Is not perhaps this very thing, which is taught about contradiction, the most striking example of a contradiction? No; as certainly as it is that, so little could it, as what it is, strike one, so long as one, out of sheer logic and critique, failed to recognize the essence of one’s own thinking, and it was possible for *Critique itself* (pp. 48, 49) to pass off being in a place, and the not-being of the very same thing in the same place, *at another time*, as the connection of *contradictorily* opposed predicates *in one and the selfsame* object (for A as A and A as Non-A) *in one* object. Only now can whoever wishes bring this too to clarity in himself. The *Omnis* and *Omne* is a mere section of human imagining-into the world, arbitrary for thinking *as thinking*, under the eternal universality of thinking itself, as the *Quidam* and *Quoddam*, the *Hic* and *Hoc*, are such a section.—I am amazed, as I write this down, even before this [p. 57] abyss, before the universality without quantity, for which every concept is too narrow and therefore destructive, every word too small.—I take up the pen again, and write once more. The *Omnis* and *Omne* is a mere section of human imagining-into the world, arbitrary for thinking *as thinking*, under the eternal universality of thinking itself, as the *Quidam* and *Quoddam*, the *Hic* and *Hoc*, are such a section; hence the *omnes homines sunt mortales* [all men are mortal] can, according to the row of the associations at hand, become also a *quidam homines sunt mortales* [some men are mortal] and a *hic homo est mortalis* [this man is mortal], just as finally, without altering thinking itself in the least, also an *omnes homines non sunt mortales* [all men are not mortal], a *quidam homines non sunt mortales* [some men are not mortal], a *hic homo non est mortalis* [this man is not mortal]. This, (even that in it which is *conflicting*, if one wishes to call it so); is no concern at all of *thinking as thinking*; the Yes is for it, as a *thinking*, like *No*; and this *Yes and No* (*existence, or non-existence*, hence *being-there*; the *Kantian* contradiction, *Crit.* p. 48) may meanwhile designate the *conflict*. If, on the other hand, one posits one or the other of these judgments (for thinking it is all one which?) then one posits therewith its infinitely-many-times [p. 58] repeatability as A, i.e. as that of a thought, hence in the row of the infinitely-many-times repeatability of A as A a Non-A will cancel all thinking, i.e. become a *contradiction*. A contradiction is therefore:

A as A and Non-A

consequently a contradiction

*Omnes homines sunt mortales* [All men are mortal]

*Quidam homines non sunt mortales* [Some men are not mortal]

(Here the linking in one thinking ceases, hence (as, after the proper explanation of the shorter expression § 12 Rem., I can say more briefly) *thinking itself* ceases. The copula is *negated*, i.e. annihilated; for a negating of the copula is an annihilating of it, no denying; *contradiction is an annihilation of the copula*).

A contradiction, further, is what up to now was not seen as a contradiction, nor could be seen, the

*Quidam homines sunt mortales* [Some men are mortal]

*Non-Quidam homines sunt mortales*, [Non-some men are mortal]

if, what in the *Quidam* is only thought, the *thought Quidam* (just so the *omnis*: *non-omnis*) and not a mere, for thinking *as* [p. 59] *thinking* quite arbitrary section of human imagining-into the world, next to which then again a *Non-Quidam* can exist infinitely many times repeatable, is meant by it.

A contradiction, further, the

*Hic homo est mortalis* [This man is mortal]

*Non-Hic homo est mortalis*, [Non-this man is mortal]

if, what in the *Hic* is merely thought, the *thought Hic*, and not a mere section, quite arbitrary for thinking *as thinking*, of human imagining-into the world, is meant, next to which, to be sure, then again indeterminably many *Non-hic homo est mortalis* [Non-this man is mortal] can exist without hindrance. On that first *Non-Hic*, posited in the row of the infinitely-many-times repeatability of the *Hic*, rested, to remark this in passing, the previous false expression of the misunderstood *principle of contradiction*, where it was still best expressed, as in *Maaß*. “B, *that would not be B*, is *Nothing*.” *Hic*, e.g., as B, and *Non-Hic* as Non-B, are perfectly compatible together, are therefore anything but Nothing, if only the one does not occur in the infinitely-many-times repeatability of the other, hence in the thinking of the other. Only then does thinking become a no-longer-thinking, i.e. a nothing of the [p. 60] being-linked in one thinking, a contradiction. Accordingly the principle of contradiction, already *applied to an object*, must read:

B Non-B in whose representation in whose representation

the A as A and Non-A the A as A and Non-A

occurs, is no occurs, is no thought thought. Must not, then, in accordance with all this, the *Omnitudo* for thinking *as thinking* be wholly one with the *Quoddam* and *Hoc*? But why this pointing back to what has been shown? The above *Non-Quidam* itself makes the test of the true content of the *Omnitudo*, and proves *through that which* is represented in the *Non-Quidam* (as a section of the world), that the *Omnitudo* consists merely in a representation of the *Not-some*; for how else could that *Non-Quidam* (as section) take the place of the representation of the *Omnis* in thinking? But here there manifests itself yet a “hitherto wholly unnoticed” remarkable thing.

The above

*Non-quidam homines sunt mortales* [Non-some men are mortal]

turns over for one, namely, in the act of thinking, just as well into the representation of a [p. 61] *Omnis homo est mortalis* [Every man is mortal]

as into the representation of a

*Nullus homo est mortalis* [No man is mortal]

that is, in other words, the altered quality of the object (*Some*) alters for me, to be sure, also the world-section in which I am to assert something of it, and gives me precisely thereby to recognize that it, *as object*, by means of the difference of quality it has brought with it, may dominate this world-section. But it fills it, as altered quality of the object, with nothing yet; but demands *apprehensions*, which must first decide upon the *Omnis* or *Nullus*. It is therefore not only thinking, *as thinking*, which can decide nothing about the difference of quality; but also the object as object brings with it, to be sure, the possibility of this difference of quality, but does not itself determine it; rather it is blind without apprehensions.—But if once the quality is determined by apprehensions: then the quality determined by apprehensions dominates the quantity too. This will be confirmed if we take the above *Non-Hic* into account. Even the above

*Non-Hic homo est mortalis* [Non-this man is mortal] [p. 62] will, namely, be able to turn over for us in thinking either into the representation of a

*Nullus homo est mortalis* [No man is mortal]

or into the representation of a

*Omnis homo est mortalis* [Every man is mortal]

that is, in other words, the altered quality of the object (*this one*) alters for me, to be sure, also the world-section in which I am to assert something of it, but it fills it, as altered quality of the object, with nothing yet; but leaves what in it does not yet rest upon an actual apprehension to further experience.
