The minus in the middle, *the slumber*, the *mere feeling*, makes a plus possible from the one side and from the other. If on the material (by virtue of the alternation under a rule, *as this alternation takes place in a certain individuum*), hence on the *individual* apprehensions of a certain head, everything that is material in it is obliterated; then not only can the ( − B<sup>3</sup> + b<sup>3</sup> B ) *as rule*, but A itself too with its highest and last plus, enter his consciousness; he cognizes the *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, together with the rule which it created for the universe, he *wakes*, and is a *human being* (*effective*). If however on the material (by virtue of the alternation under a rule, *as* [p. 263] *this alternation takes place in a certain individuum*), hence on the *individual* apprehensions of a certain head, not everything that is material in it is obliterated (in order then first to let this material attach itself again to the − B<sup>3</sup> + b<sup>3</sup> B, under a cognized A): then he dreams, when he speculates, seeks under an (− B<sup>2</sup>) standing everywhere in front with him (*as human being*), cause, ground and *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν in the conditions of similarity, coexistence and opposition, entangles himself therefore, for the sake of the (− B<sup>2</sup>) standing in front, in sheer contradictions, as, when he acts, in sheer transgressions, whereas the animal, for lack of a (− B<sup>2</sup>) accompanying its consciousness, is guided quite surely to its goal through similarity, coexistence and opposition. What even the merely *historical* cognition of a God and of his will, without having been guided to him by one’s own thinking, what then the taking up of this *historical* cognition into his *individual* feeling, as the mainspring of his actions, must contribute to the *moral* emancipation of man from the condition of an unhappy middle-thing, and how pernicious therefore the crooked course of a [p. 264] speculation which *denied the Good as God’s Will* might finally become, is plain of itself. *Moral* emancipation of man from the condition of an unhappy middle-thing between man and animal, *after and through* God’s will, consequently surely also chiefly *through* religion, is *freedom*; and to attain by the philosophical means to a high degree of this moral freedom, (to rid oneself of the *arbitrium servum*, ἐνθένδε ἐκεῖσε φεύγειν ὅτι τάχιστα· φυγὴ δὲ ὁμοίωσις Θεῷ κατὰ τὸ δυνατόν. *Plato*) I know no more excellent writing than *Ehlers’s doctrine of human freedom*, together with the works of the *Stoics*. — To B or to *extension, as extension*, the waking monad attains (in its representation) under the condition of *coexistence*; to (− B) under the condition of opposition; to (+ b) under the condition of similarity; just as its life itself *in its individuality* under the condition of coexistence to B or to *extension, as extension*: under the condition of attraction and repulsion to (− B); and under the condition of affinity to (+ b), hence under sheer conditions *on the part of the* [p. 265] material *already standing under a rule*, attained to individuality, *as individuality*. These conditions, now, must indeed be obliterated from within, as conditions of life, consequently as coexistence of certain parts, as attraction and repulsion, as affinity, even in the animal’s consciousness; otherwise no consciousness arises. But since the material nevertheless asserts its existence: so they show themselves for it as *conditions of representing*, or, as it is called, as laws of imagination (of the imagining-into [*Hineinbildung*] the world), and indeed in such a way that the coexistence under B is a law of representation as well as of memory *of the same name*, attraction and repulsion (under − B) a law of representation as well as of memory under the name of *opposition*, affinity (under + b) a law of representation as well as of memory under the name of *similarity*. Everything that comes to it from outside is consequently, even in the animal, not brought to it otherwise than under these conditions; the minus of a becoming-conscious of the material, *as material*, of which it (*individualiter*) consists, only makes room for the plus of a becoming-aware from outside. Just so it stands with us too, except that our apprehensions then reciprocally bring back the represented, not only as something [p. 266] represented, but also again as something thought, into our consciousness. If it comes back as something thought: then it comes back, let it be what it may, as (B − B + b), hence as a change under a rule, and reveals to us in this (B − B + b) the rule or necessary determination of every atom, to yield itself, under the requisite conditions of coexistence, of attraction and repulsion, of affinity, as soon as the change under a rule shall bring them about, to organism. What, according to the above results, (B − B + b) in us, as set into an image, yet into no *proper* concept, indicates, precisely that (B − B + b) indicates, set in *such* an atom, which the change under a rule has not yet brought under those conditions of coexistence etc., under which it must come, not only *virtualiter* but also *effective*, to the ( − B + b B ) in it. Just as therefore the continuing existence of the material on the one side is the indispensable condition for the application of the rule in an *individual* consciousness, even in the *individual* consciousness of man: so the continuing existence of the material on the other side is again the [p. 267] unavoidable obstacle, that the rule, present *virtualiter* everywhere and in every atom, nevertheless cannot be applied *effective* everywhere and in every atom, but must wait in the change under a rule for the conditions of coexistence etc., until it can come *effective* to the ( − B + b B ) in it.

Only man has also already *effective* power over the being of this atom (of a *bound* monad), by expressing in a *judgment* its being ( − B + b B ), determining it not only as object, but also as a *This*, as e.g. in the judgment, *this wall is white*, which is nothing other than *a particular of the multiplicity* of the material (white), manifested in man in the rule of the universal, which expresses the formula of the essences ( − B + b B ). If now precisely this rule, after the change under a rule, manifests itself *effective* also in the atoms themselves, of which this wall consists, if the change under a rule brings about the requisite conditions of coexistence etc.: then there happens [p. 268] to these atoms what *virtualiter* lay in them already as atoms, and what my judgment (my rule for them) also as their rule already *effective* stated, they are *unbound*; their slumber begins in a plant, their dreaming in an animal, their waking in man. The material lying under them gives then the *particular of the multiplicity* (the species, subspecies, variety, individuality, indeed the *genus* itself, *as it has hitherto* been understood *in the logics as a mark-genus*, forth; ( − B + b B ) on the other hand the organism, *as organism*, yields the *genus*, *as genus*, or *as possibility of all genera*, the concept, *as possibility of all* concepts, i.e. of all complexions of sensible marks). — But it seems nevertheless as if we, over other discussions, had quite forgotten our above *Either-or*! It is not forgotten; but, through the stepwise course of the analysis, its discussion has been prepared, or rather first made possible. The material, or the disjunction *with* the *hypothesis*, had first also to be brought fully into the clear, after already before the disjunction *without hypothesis*, thinking in its necessarily disjunctive application [p. 269] to the *form* of the material, had been given satisfaction; *and now first are we also*, *with the accidental*, yet therein with what is necessarily determined, near the goal; the circular path will close. Above stands in his *Being* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, A (*as A in A through A*). As unavoidable hypothesis of his efficacy stands under him the *existence* of a material. A, (*as A in A, through A*) annihilates the material down to its form, so as, in the world of an all-wise Author, something lets itself be annihilated. In this way an *object* arises. The possibility becomes in a (− B); the actuality becomes in a (B): where (B − B) is posited, there with this also (+ b) is posited; the form of the universal *must* then also become form of the particular: a system of things arises. Possibility, actuality, necessity are in *this* region, on this side of the object and of the genus, *as genus*, hence indeed downward in the row and into the system of things, but nevertheless still abstracted from the material, *as material, as wholly inseparable*, given. What is possible must also be actual; what is actual must also be possible: what is actual and possible, and possible and actual, must be necessary. What is logically true must also be physically true; what is physically true [p. 270] must also be logically true. B at the object cannot take place without (− B), and (− B) at the object cannot without B, and (B − B) not without (+ b). But B is not on that account (− B), and (− B) is not on that account B; and (+ b) is neither B alone, nor (− B) alone, but is the immediate necessary consequence of both in their connection. Here a *disjunction*; but a disjunction *without hypothesis*; it is called *possibility, actuality*, (B − B); it is *absolutely* in regard to everything that stands under it, *absolutely* in regard to the whole world-system: it grounds the *Being of the second order*; *relatively* is it only in regard to the *Being of the first order*, to the ὄν κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, *relatively* is it only in regard to *God*. If I see into possibility in mathematics: then I also see into actuality and necessity; and if I see into the actuality and necessity of a mathematical proposition: then I also see into its possibility. Just so in morals, where the actuality determined through possibility is called (necessary) *obligatoriness* (*obligatio*). Demonstrating itself and in general taken, what does it mean otherwise than to demonstrate through the possibility the actuality, and through the actuality the possibility, [p. 271] but through possibility and actuality the necessity of a truth? The ground, conditioned by nothing *in the world-system* any more, of all possible *Either — or* is hereby discussed. For it is that opposition (ἐναντιότης), which, separated by the opposing and yet indissolubly bound, two taken together, promotes to complete unity, and of which the one as well as the other stands under the absolute, uninterrupted influence of A, as of the essence of essences, without the one nonetheless ever becoming that which the other is, nor this that which that is. It is that without which no subject with its predicate in logic, — and without which no world-system, *as system*, would be possible. Everything that is to obtain an existence through *Being* plunges over this two-part cataract into life; above it thrones and guides the stream, and reveals itself and its world-system, *as system*, to man — *the Eternal Unnameable*. But this ground, conditioned by nothing within the world-system any more, of all possible *Either — or* can, without contradiction, just as little itself again be an *Either — or*, as *these* are, just as little as the *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν and the material, each considered by itself, [p. 272] could without contradiction already be an object. No, it must rather, as a ground, conditioned by nothing within the world-system any more, of every possible alternative, admit itself and *in its essence* no *exclusive* alternative any more, if every possible *exclusive* alternative is to be capable of being fully determined through it. In it must rather, as said, the possibility include also the actuality, possibility and actuality also the necessity; in short, it must be disjunction *without hypothesis* (without a further *conditio sine qua non within the world-system*); otherwise the disjunctions *with the hypothesis* within the world-system would have absolutely no firm hold on it. For if in the change under a rule, hence in the world-system, the change *only as change* stirs up the rule in the *becoming-conscious* (I say merely: in the becoming-conscious) of a subject: then it stirs it up, *as change*, in the quality of a mere unavoidable *hypothesis*, in order to arrive at the becoming-conscious of the rule. The rule ( − B + b B ) awakens thus *inwardly* in the becoming-conscious, there where it is given to a subject, as *ingredient* of its becoming-conscious, *necessarily*; it [p. 273] comes to the thought of possibility (− B) and actuality (B), and of actuality determined through possibility (− B + b). But that which stirred the rule in this way appeared in the becoming-conscious only as *change*, even though it is change under a rule, and *must be*. Did it appear only as change, then it appeared only as material; and did it appear only as material: then it appeared 1) only as a coexistence *without rule*, (without its *ground*), hence as *accidental* coexistence; it came about, we say, accidentally. Since now however the rule is nevertheless there inwardly in the becoming-conscious: so the coexistence itself will *have to* be made by us into the rule, or into the ground, into actuality, into the B in that which came into our becoming-conscious only as change (before one instructs us better). Actuality is posited into a piece of our world-imagining lying just now before us, into an *existence*, into a *Kantian* category of this name, which in *Kant* still has the singular quality that it, without being afflicted with space and time (*as a category*), nevertheless exhibits an existence. What is merely the indispensable hypothesis of an actuality for us, is thus itself made by us into actuality. But that by which the rule inwardly [p. 274] and in our becoming-conscious, only *as* stirred *through* a *change* from outside, appears in this becoming-conscious 2) as coexistence *under an opposition*; it might, it seems to us, either have happened or not, might either now or another time, either sooner or later, either here or there, either so or otherwise, either to a higher or lower degree and measure, have occurred; especially since our memory (the coexistence *in us*) brings forward a multitude of opposite cases in our consciousness. The coexistence, *falsely* raised by us already to actuality, or to the ground, which yet is merely *hypothesis* (*conditio sine qua non*) of an actuality for us, is thus now also fully honored by us with the honor of a *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν. The Either-or, with which it was overlaid in our representation, *as a mere representation*, rises in this representation, *but also only in it alone*, now fully above it, and goes, as *Fortuna*, as *Epicurean chance, which itself rules the coexistence (as the supposed ground) everywhere and absolutely, (as its prius κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν)*, as possibility *without* [p. 275] *actuality*, as possibility, posited for a mere supposed *accident*, as logically possible? but physically impossible, as hippocentaur and as chimera, as *Kantian* category of a possibility to be derived first from the wavering of a judgment, as *Reinholdian*, grown equally to the Either — or, *sans rime et sans raison*, arbitrariness etc., up over the universe. What is merely the unavoidable hypothesis (*conditio sine qua non*) *for us*, in order to stir up in us a *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, an absolute, that even rules the ground (*id, quod est fundamenti loco ponendum* [that which is to be put in the place of the ground]) too, that is itself made by us into the *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, *chance, arbitrariness*, (as imagination, *which first presses upon a thinking*), the *seemingly accidental* in a change, which nevertheless stands under a rule, and must stand under a rule, hence *can* admit nothing accidental; for otherwise there would be no world-system, *as system*, and the saying of Scripture: *without God’s, your Father’s, will not a hair falls from your head*; contains in this respect more pure reason than the whole *Critique of pure as well as practical reason*. But that by which the rule in our becoming-conscious only as [p. 276] stirred through a change from outside, appears in this becoming-conscious 3) as coexistence *under a similarity* (outwardly affinity). This similarity is mere *conditio sine qua non* for us, in order to arrive at the equality (identity) of the form of the material, how this form under A, according to everything that its three dimensions of space admitted, multiplies itself in itself, or, in other words, how it, as actuality, through possibility under A, is everywhere completely determined. In short, this similarity is mere *conditio sine qua non* for us, in order to arrive at (+ b), at the cause in every thing. Since now however after preceding coexistence *in general*, and preceding coexistence *under an opposition*, (attraction and repulsion, alteration of coexistence, motion), — the coexistence *under an affinity* really *on the part of the material* is the hypothesis without further hypothesis, in order to be able to posit therein immediately a (+ b), hence the identity of a form: so it all depends on whether the coexistence entering our consciousness *under a similarity* is also really a coexistence *under an affinity on the part of the present material itself*, (character of the acute heads; *harmonia* [p. 277] *vel quasi præstabilita* [or as it were pre-established]), or not, (character of the philosophical etc. cranks, *ils aperçoivent les verités de la Geometrie, mai elle ne leur aprend point à peser les probabilités. Ils ont pris leur pli, ils raisonneront de travers toute leur vie, et j’en suis faché pour eux, Volt. Quest. sur l’ Encic. Art. Esprit faux*) [They perceive the truths of geometry, but it does not teach them to weigh probabilities. They have taken their bent; they will reason wrongly all their lives, and I am sorry for them]. Is it a coexistence under an affinity *on the part of the present material itself*: then it is a *conditio sufficiens*, an applicable material-*medius*, in order to stir up (+ b) *there in us*, where it also outside us, under a hypothesis without further hypothesis *on the part of the material*, under its sensible attributes, is posited immediately and *effective* in a present object, where we consequently can determine applicably, and without further Either — or, the actuality thereon categorically through the possibility, (*posita conditione sufficiente, ex attributo sensuali, qua attributo* τῷ (+ b), *ponitur effectus*) [the sufficient condition being posited, from the sensible attribute, as attribute of the (+ b), the effect is posited]. As well as one can therefore say: *L ponit M*, just as well can one also say: *Non L (conditio non sufficiens) non ponit M*, of which indeed our hitherto best logics (*Ploucquet Elementa Philos. Contempl. p. 71,* Maaß l. c. p. 11) assume the opposite, [p. 278] and in the second case merely had to say: *Non M, non ponit L.* — What accordingly is merely the unavoidable hypothesis of a *cause*, of a *genus*, of a *concept* (as of the *possibility* of any complexion of sensible apprehensions) for us, the affinity of the material marks from outside, and the complexion precisely *of these* material marks from outside, *which have affinity*, also from inside through our manner of imagining-into [*Hineinbildung*] the world, as well as the imagining-into of the world in us, that is itself made by us into the cause, (until we are instructed better); in that we say e.g. categorically: the germ or the seed is the cause of the plant, further that our earth is a planet, is the cause of its motion around the sun. (Now Hume’s objections against the concept of cause, *without all Kantian critique*, might have been sufficiently met, and at the same time my above, contradictory-sounding, expression of demonstrative syllogisms *in empiricism* be justified. The *Humean* habit, the routine, can indeed ultimately lead to the affinities of a present world-section, hence to the immediate stirring of the (+ b) in us, there where it also from outside *effective* lies, consequently to demonstrability *in empiricism*, *in the routine*, [p. 279] as to the *conditio sine qua non* for this kind of demonstrability). — But the *Either — or* in our animal becoming-conscious (in our manner of imagining-into the world), is now only dispatched *as deception*, without its usefulness for truth itself having been already wholly discussed; hence it is not yet fully dispatched. For even deception namely can in a world-system, *as system*, not be absolute, but it must be merely relative, i.e. *it must make room for truth*, as the immortal *Leibniz* has proved of evil in the world in general, that it becomes each time the mother of the good. On humanity’s most important concern, on God, leads the *Either — or* the thinking man *apodictically*, this we saw. On the possibility of an application of his thinking in general to the cognition of the world of the senses, leads the *Either — or* every man (as unavoidable hypothesis of this possibility of an application of it), this remains still to be demonstrated. It is not to be denied that *this Either — or*, which is not, as one hitherto supposed, a law of understanding and thinking, but lies in our imagination, all sophistry, all conflict of philosophical systems, all objections of skepsis, [p. 280] and indeed each of these pieces *as a supposed* work of *thinking itself*, it has occasioned. It is further obvious that without the taking up of *this* Either — or into the form of thinking itself, no condemnation of this form, recently experienced by us, to a mere blind-work would have been possible. Yet incomprehensible might it seem to one that one should have been speaking so long of a form, hence of something unalterable, of the essence of things, and yet in doing so *was able* to take an *exclusive* Either — or, even into the form and the essence of thinking, *as of a thinking*; but most incomprehensible of all, that the actual *formal-philosopher* of our century was capable of treating the form, hence the essence, without which things would be nothing, in such a way, as if it were a mere non-essence, which only through intuitions gets a content, an essence, a sense and a meaning for us. What, from page 82 on, is reported as new in the *Critique of Pure Reason*, must therefore appear to a sober age, which no longer has a mind to let its own reason have the staff broken over it, as the most ridiculous satire on the philosopher himself who wrote it. Not even in man’s imagination [p. 281] is there, in spite of *its* either—or, such an *absolute* deception as that of our *reason*, in *Kant*, which would not even be usable as an instrument (organon) for something real, *l. c.* This will soon appear, if we first of all reassure ourselves again of the whole series of our thoughts belonging here. A being-represented *outside-, beside- and after-one-another* is a *conditio sine qua non* if in our thinking a *something* is to be represented; this was proved above. This outside-, beside- and after-one-another is and remains, *as form*, indestructible; but because it is an outside-, beside- and after-one-another, it is the indestructible form of a thing which is not already everything that it is *in and through itself*, in which therefore, *as in this thing*, (abstracting from its *form*) a temporal *becoming*, a *growing*, a *diminishing*, a *difference of degree* takes place. It is material, and alternates *as material*; but its alternation now stands under a rule, which states the formula of the essences; and if it stands under a rule: then in it, as alternation *under a rule*, no either—or occurs any longer; so much is evident. But through *being* under a rule the material, *as material*, has nevertheless not lost its existence; (see above!)

[p. 282] If the material has not lost its existence, *as material*: then its existence will now manifest itself in the rule either as an existence through which being in us is merely stirred up, or else as a being through which an existence in us is determined. If the material manifests to us its existence *merely as an existence*: then it manifests it to us *without rule on its part*, hence with the possible opposition of a being-there, etc. (simple representation). If the material manifests to us its existence under a being; (if we annihilate in thinking the material, *as material*, down to the form): then it manifests it to us with the rule on its part, hence without possible opposition of a being-there, being-other, etc., in that it *is there*. In the former case the existence itself is indeed no deception; but the possible opposition of a being-there, being-other, etc., *in that it is there precisely now in this manner*, is a deception. But it is a deception which becomes usable as an instrument (organon) for something supremely real; in that through the existence, as no deception (in so far as it is taken merely for an existence), the *actuality* in a consciousness, to which it is conferred as actuality; through the possible being-there, however, of that which nevertheless *is there*, *as a deception indeed*, [p. 283] the *possibility*, (hence actually something supremely real) in a consciousness, to which it is conferred as possibility, is consequently stirred up through both together (B − B), and finally, through the coming-together of existence and being-there in our imagination, (as outside us through attraction and repulsion) like adds itself to like, hence, for the stirring-up of the (+ b) in our becoming-conscious, the necessary hypothesis, without further hypothesis on the part of the material, is wholly prepared. This last, there, when like joins like, therefore gives us the *categorical* assertion about things, for which we have found the *conditionem sine qua non* for all conditions, the sensible attribute, in that likeness, and gave our hitherto existing logics 1) one of their so-called supreme laws of thought, of the title: *nihil sit sine ratione sufficiente* [let nothing be without sufficient reason], which the *Kantians* have even transformed into a subjective condition of the possibility of a thinking in general, *as a thinking*, (*Jacob l. c.* p. 38, §. 82., *Kiesewetter l. c.* p. 10, §. 17, cf. pp. 11, 12) and which *must* run thus: *posita conditione sufficiente quoad materiam, necessario ponitur causa* (+ b), *et posita causa* (+ b) *sub conditione sufficiente quoad materiam*, (the change [p. 284] under a rule unbinds the bound monad), *necessario ponitur effectus* [given a sufficient condition as to the material, the cause (+ b) is necessarily posited, and given the cause (+ b) under a sufficient condition as to the material, the effect is necessarily posited]; (in its purity it will have to run thus: *posito* (B − B), *necessario ponitur* (+ b), *vel virtualiter*, *ubi deest conditio sufficiens quoad materiam*, *vel effective*, *ubi adest conditio sufficiens, quoad materiam* [given (B − B), (+ b) is necessarily posited, either virtually, where a sufficient condition as to the material is lacking, or effectively, where a sufficient condition as to the material is present]). There was given 2) to our hitherto existing logics their material-*medius* for *categorical* syllogisms. The first, on the other hand (the existence, namely, as no deception in so far as it is taken merely for an existence,) gives us the *categorical* assertion about things of which we were *eyewitnesses*. But as to the coming-together of existence and being-there (being-other) in our imagination, in particular: it is quite properly the brief sum of all that must happen on the part of the material in order to make objects of the senses *cognizable* to us through thinking. Not only is it the outcome of that coming-together that likenesses thereby join with likenesses, and thereby procure for the (+ b), the possibility of a causal connection in us, the requisite foundation; but it serves in general every one of the supposedly pure laws of thought hitherto set forth in the logics as the indispensable hypothesis. For if the existence, with the imagination-opposition of the possible being-there of a thing, [p. 285] which nevertheless *is here* and hence *can*not be there, has once stirred up in us the actuality and possibility (B − B), and likenesses (complexions of sensible marks, first images, afterwards concepts in the ordinary sense) have thereupon, because (B − B) necessarily produces a (+ b), under this (+ b) now necessarily ensuing, also necessarily been drawn together in us: then, *reciprocally*, all existence comes now to stand with us under B, all being-there or being-other under (− B), every complexion of sensible marks under (+ b), in such a way that we judge or *cognize* all existence and being-there (or being-other) according to (B − B), all complexions of sensible marks under likenesses, according to (+ b); (the latter gives us the manifold *forces*, or complexions of sensible marks under (+ b), in which, *as mere complexions of sensible marks*, we falsely believe we have found the key to the innermost of nature). B now, in so far as it is B, cannot be (− B); and (− B), in so far as it is (− B), cannot be B; (+ b), however, can be posited neither through B alone, nor through (− B) alone; but must first be posited through both together. In accordance with this we now judge or *cognize* 1) that the existence or presence according to space and time (thus [p. 286] that which stands with us under B), *in so far as it is here*, is not also absent according to space and time, past or future, consequently, regarded by us as B, not also at once (− B); but that also, conversely, the absent according to space and time, the past or the future (thus that which stands with us under − B), *in so far as it is absent, past or future*, is not also present according to space and time, consequently regarded by us as (− B) cannot also at once be B; (*idem non potest simul esse et non esse* [the same thing cannot at once be and not be]). Were B, as B, at once also (− B): then A, as A, which produced both, and manifests itself in both, *but also essentially distinguished both*, would have at once not to distinguish both essentially, consequently to deny its necessity as *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, i.e. to become Non A out of A, which contradicts itself. If the existence, as standing with us under B, *in so far as it stands under this B*, cannot also be regarded by us as being-there or being-other, because otherwise B, in so far as it is B, would also at once have to be (− B): then it follows that a sensible mark which once is here, *in so far as it is here*, cannot possibly also at once be regarded as a sensible mark which would be *there*, hence not *here*. If, then, [p. 287] to such a thing, as this thing, a sensible mark is now just ascribed by someone: then this ascription, just now occurring, of this sensible mark must hold, with him who ascribed it to it, for a *being* (actuality = B), until perhaps the existence of this sensible mark in this thing (in alteration under a rule) has become being-there or being-other (never however absolute not-being), although another is not compelled to judge precisely so with respect to this *lent* kind of being; but rather arbitrary affirming or denying, as the Critique calls it, may begin here. In every possible judgment (about things that fall within the world-system), be it affirmative or negative, there is accordingly expressed 1) a being of the first order (a thinking) 2) a being of the second order (B − B + b), 3) a lent being of existence or being-there under (B − B), whereby he who, with respect to the *lent* being asserted therein, posits under B the judgment already determined in its essence by Nos. 1 and 2, will affirm it, but he who in this respect posits it under (− B) will deny the same. Since now all in a world-system must be determined under (B − B), and it within the [p. 288] world-system there is no third *determining* thing (+ b itself is not determined thereby): then only from this follows the proposition, which unavoidably needs the foregoing *opposition* in the imagination as its indispensable hypothesis; *to every thing a certain mark either accrues or does not accrue*. If one has, then, once gathered together empirically all that which, according to our manner of imagining-into the world, belongs to the existence as well as the being-there of an object of the senses at hand, if one knows, e.g., once all the primary colors, and one of these primary colors is in question in the case of a certain existence (hence under B): then one need only, through the imagination-law of the opposition, make present to oneself all the variations hitherto observed of the being-there or being-other of the primary colors (under − B), in order to be able to say with certainty, either this, or that, or a third, etc. primary color must accrue to the existence at hand, (objects of the senses under B); for through (B − B) everything in a world-system is completely determined. But notwithstanding that B, as B, cannot also at once be regarded as (− B), and conversely: still neither can take place without the other. Since, then, e.g., the denier posits under (− B) that which the affirmer [p. 289] posits under B: then the denier too must have set in front of his (− B) a B (as objects), and the affirmer must have attached to his B a (− B), as objects. This means, in other words: the being *lent* to an object in language or during apprehension indeed admits, to be sure, of the alternative of existence (under B), or being-there (under − B), and is for that very reason merely lent; but existence as well as being-there, *regarded in and for themselves*, must nevertheless be as possible as actual, and as actual as possible; what is logically true must also be physically true, and what is physically true must also be logically true; whether it be, *according to the measure of alteration under a rule*, here or there, in the past, present or future (prognosticating), only ripe for *effective* emergence in the series of phenomena; this does not matter at all for being in itself (within the world-system); enough that no actuality without possibility, and no possibility without actuality, *can* take place, so long as the object is not to become a non-object. *What contains no contradiction, is*; and *where accordingly in the representations of a man something is represented without contradiction, (hence thought)*, [p. 290] there the representation is *therefore correct, because with logical truth physical truth is just as inseparably bound up as (− B) with B, or as possibility must be bound up with actuality*. *What, on the other hand, is represented in the representations of a man, and is not, contains a contradiction; and is not merely because it contains a contradiction. To contain a contradiction, or to be called error, deception, deceit of the senses, is all one*; if it were, e.g., no contradiction that the sun revolved about our earth, then the sun would revolve about our earth, and the deceit of the senses, according to which we *must* believe the sun revolves about our earth, is deceit of the senses for the very reason that the belief built upon it contains a contradiction; the occasion for this contradiction, however, is that in this phenomenon too the material manifests its existence to us merely as an existence, hence without rule *on its part*, *and we now* take *the mere hypothesis* (*conditio sine qua non*), *in order to arrive at actuality*, for actuality itself; (in the animal it is no contradiction, hence no deception either; [p. 291] for the (− B²) does not come into its consciousness at all, and it must therefore hold the existence too merely for a *mere existence*). Just so it goes with us too in *sinning*; and the most powerful means against sinning is therefore to instruct one, concerning that which one is, *as man*, in something *better*; but not, concerning God, reason and world, as the sickly philosophy does, in something worse, by making, in the light French taste, Germanized through *Kant*, essence into non-essence, and non-essence into essence. *He who knows what is wrong, and does it nonetheless, to him it is sin; but if he knows it* (has taken it up hypothetically into a *thinking*, not merely into an association) and connects it with the *most real* thought of an *omnipresent* deity: then he will not sin. — Enough; and now also, finally, to the second proposition just indicated, that (+ b) can be posited neither through B alone, nor through (− B) alone, but must first be posited through both together. In accordance with this proposition men have as yet judged or cognized little; for they did not cognize the cause itself, *as cause*; but confused it with mere conditions (in the material). Now, however, in accordance with the same we cognize and judge II) [p. 292] that a category of cause, taken out absolutely from the hypothetical judgments of our understanding, consequently doubtless also all hypothetical judgments, *as hypothetical*, grounding, but at the same time, *as cause*, according to their nature and their essence, completely categorical, — could indeed have no sense and no meaning, so long as one does not accustom oneself critically to intuit them blindly; but that also, so long as it was still possible to deal thus with the cause in any school of philosophers, no philosophy at all has been possible among us. I therefore only remind further that the form of the universal would be sought in vain in the form of the particular, and consequently no world-system, *as system*, would exist, if it were not so that (+ b) can be posited neither through B alone, nor through (− B) alone, but must first be posited through both together. The essence of every atom consists, *as essence*, of an extension, *as extension, which at the same time has its purpose*, hence of (B − B + b). Only it depends on where and when the alteration under a rule will stimulate this bound monad (in the realm of lifeless nature, as we speak) to the becoming of an organic individual being. This too happens according to just the same rule, and under [p. 293] the conditions requisite in each case, on the part of the material in the macrocosm, as happens with us men, as the microcosm, when a manifestation of the essence of things is to be laid out in us, as human individuals. Naturally, the *multiplicity* of the material, which we cannot survey, which is something wholly other than *plurality*, (a million is *many*, but not *manifold*), must admit millions of varieties, under the very same (B − B + b), infinitely many times repeatable (through A), in us and outside us. The essence of all these varieties nevertheless remains for that reason one and the selfsame everywhere; otherwise it would be no essence, and the world would be no system. For at the beck of him who bears the universe, of the near and of the far, the omnipresent, eternal and all-wise, *plurality* in the unity of the form is made capable, by virtue of its three indestructible spatial dimensions, of underlying those millions of varieties, as *ground* (B), and as *their* respective (+ b). To know the possible sum of the pluralities in the unity of the form, which are permitted through its three spatial dimensions, would therefore mean as much as to know the *cause* (Ur-sache) (+ b) of each kind of those millions of varieties? For drawing and outline are indeed, as *Sulzer* [p. 294] says, the very *soul* of painting. — — — On the *object*, on the *This*, on the *either—or*, therefore, depends the philosophy of the system of things, *as world-beings*; so much, I believe, this remark has *demonstrated*. The reckoning has been carried through to the last; and now I can therefore also conclude the remark itself. I do so with a brief compilation of its principal results:

That which grounds the possibility of the object (B − B) cannot again be object (B − B), but must be *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, *God*, (*manifestatio Essentiae divinae* [manifestation of the divine Essence]) and under him must be, as hypothesis, *material*.

That which grounds the possibility of the *This*, whose hypothesis in the material is the coexistence *under an affinity*, cannot be B alone, and not (− B) alone, but must be B, determined through (− B), = (+ b).

That which grounds the possibility of the exclusive *either—or*, whose hypothesis in the material is the coexistence *under an opposition*, cannot itself again be exclusive, but must, without possible exclusion, be together, hence (B − B).

[p. 295] B (− B + b) *virtualiter*, (*sine conditione sufficiente quoad materiam*); *bound* monad in alteration under a rule.

B (− B + b) *effective*, (*cum conditione sufficiente quoad materiam*); *slumbering* monad, essence of the *plant*.

B (− B² + b²) Essence of the *animal*; enters the consciousness of the animal as ( + b² B ); *dreaming* monad.

B (− B³ + b³) Essence of *man, as man*; enters the consciousness of man either as ( − B² + b² B ); — *animal-man*;—or as ( − B³ + b³ B ), where it comes in him, theoretically as well as practically, to the recognition of *A as A, in A, through A*; *waking* monad; *man*.

These formulas will remain, so long as a cognition of that which was thought by *Pythagoras*, (perhaps [p. 296] from the Orient) under numbers, stirred up again by *Leibniz*’s lofty spirit, but also vanishing meteorically with him—the *law of identity*—, consequently a cognition of thinking, consequently a philosophy, remains among men; this I know, and I bow in the dust, over which they may pronounce the *et me aliquando vixisse* [and that I too once lived], even if it mingles my ashes again with the ashes of my fathers. How the powers in the above scales and *consequentiis immediatis* are to be placed, where the latter relate to man, *as man*, the knowledgeable reader will gauge for himself. It would be a small trouble to enter the numbers there properly. But there I could only divine powers, not yet *think* them; they therefore do not yet belong there. — Thus it developed with me: thus I have thought it; thus written it down: and thus shall it also be printed. I shall alter nothing, but only strike out what my I may here and there have inspired in me in displeasure, and what my *reflection* finds unfair. — — That neither *thinking as thinking*, nor the object, *as object*, nor the This, *as This*, admits of any other than a *categorical* relation, hence of *no difference of relation* at all, I hope now [p. 297] no longer to need to prove. With modality, then, may this Outline conclude.

3) The copula in *hypothetical* judgments is absolutely necessary and without difference of modality; or, what in every possible *hypothetical* judgment is merely thought (hence is formally and properly *logical* in the hitherto accepted sense of the word), that is absolutely necessary and without difference of modality.

*Rem.* Just this holds of the *object* and of the *This* (B − B + b) in every *hypothetical* judgment; both are absolutely necessary and without difference of modality. The little word *If* designates merely the hypothesis on the part of the material, over which one would gladly arrive at the object as a *This*, hence at the *cause* (Ur-sache) of a phenomenon; hence in the best logics before *Kant* hypothetical syllogisms were already counted among the *syllogismi imperfecti* [imperfect syllogisms], (*Ploucquet l. c.* p. 58, 59). If now the hypothesis on the part of the material is a *conditio sine qua non*, for all the remaining conditions, if it is provided with the requisite *sufficiency*: then *that which is inferred from it will also be correct*. Only the *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν has a consequence [p. 298] without any hypothesis already in and through itself, (*A, as A, through A*). — How our hitherto existing logics dealt with *ground and consequence* in hypothetical propositions is evident from the following passage in *Kiesewetter l. c.* p. 50, §. 109. “If, then, one posits the ground, one must also posit the consequence; but not conversely, if one posits the consequence, one must also posit the ground, *for a thing can be cognized from several grounds*.” This is supposed to be logic, and indeed pure general logic, when one concedes to thinking the necessary positing of the consequence through the ground, but no longer the necessary positing of the ground through the consequence, since there can, after all, be *several grounds*? — Is, then, an empirical condition a ground? A logic which makes empirical conditions into the ground, and which then concedes to the *consequence* the apodictic character of its connection with the ground, but denies to the ground the apodictic character *of its* connection with the consequence, in that it says: *if one posits the consequence, one need not also posit the ground*;” is it a logic? A logic which, however, then nevertheless asserts again (p. 110, §. 235): “if the [p. 299] consequent (the consequence) is *cancelled*, then the antecedent (the ground in the hitherto existing sense) must also be *cancelled*, *for the antecedent stands with the consequent in the relation of ground and consequence*” — a logic that thus *customarily* gives back with the other hand, *according to circumstances*, what it has taken with the one: is it a logic? If the ground is there, then the consequence is there; and if the consequence is there, then the ground is there. This a logic that is to deserve its name must hold fast to, even if it did not know how it is then to get along with certain apprehensions. The arranging of certain apprehensions must conform to logic; not logic to the special view of certain apprehensions: and where both come into conflict, there the settling must therefore not be sought at the expense of logic, and by means of a restriction, i.e. annihilation, of its *apodictic* assertions; but the apparent conflict must lie in an (illogical) confusion of our representations of things, as e.g. here in the confusion of the ground with mere sensible conditions. If it comes, in alteration under [p. 300] a rule, to the possible manifestation of the ground *under certain sensible conditions*, (certain affinities): then it comes *eo ipso* also to the possible manifestation of its consequences under these sensible conditions. The consequence posits the ground everywhere as well as the ground the consequence. — If I know further, e.g., once, and know it *apodictically*, that the form of the universal *must* also be the form of the particular, if the world is to be no chaos but a system, and the atom will not fit itself to this apodictic assertion: then I should proceed very absurdly if I wished on that account to restrict again, i.e. annihilate, the apodictic assertion in the case of the atom. No, the atom *must* then bear the form of the universal in itself, like the greatest of all suns; and if it should ever not be so with it: then it is not its fault but mine, in that I take the lack of manifold sensible marks in it as if it therefore also lacked its share in the form of the universal, that is to say, as if it did not belong as well as I to a world-system, *if it is to be system, no chaos*. (No concept, no word, no proof, not even [p. 301] a connection in sensations, no sympathy, would be possible if the form of the universal were not also at the same time form of the particular. *Kant* treated form (the essence) in the *Critique of Pure Reason* as a mere *façon*, and, because the *façon* of a tobacco-pipe bowl would be nothing without the tobacco-pipe bowl itself, he judged quite transcendentally that form first becomes something through its content; for the material, he said, is what first gives it sense and meaning. How he proceeds in this with the object itself, according to an *omnitudo* appended to it, by means of logical conversion, is to be read in the *Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 283, where one will in any case find exceedingly much that is remarkable about possibility, actuality and necessity, from p. 265—288, as well as about the ὂν κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν from p. 113—116. This *per parenthesin*).

4) The copula in *disjunctive* judgments is absolutely necessary and without difference of modality; or, what in every possible *disjunctive* judgment is *merely* thought (hence formally and properly *logical* in the [p. 302] hitherto accepted sense of the word), that is absolutely necessary and without difference of modality.

*Rem.* The same holds of the object and of the *This* (B − B + b) in every *disjunctive* judgment; both are absolutely necessary and without difference of modality. That one must begin with disjunctive judgments in logic, if it is to come forth into applied thinking, because without a preceding disjunction (even without the *exclusive* either-or) no categorical and no hypothetical judgment would be possible in empiricism, is evident of itself from what was said under the Rem. to No. 3 of this §. It is striking that the *Kantians* too (*Jacob* l. c., pp. 83, 85, *Kiesewetter* l. c., pp. 54, 55) concede to hypothetical as well as to disjunctive judgments no other than *apodictic* modality: not because they had observed in this the *copula, as copula*, the *object as object*, the *This as This*, in its connection with the first two; otherwise they would have had to assert the same of *categorical* judgments too; but because in hypothetical and disjunctive judgments the relation of the ground to its consequence [p. 303] is expressed, but the ground posits its consequence *necessarily*. Yet if the ground now posits its consequence necessarily, how could they, a couple of lines earlier, nevertheless ascribe to hypothetical judgments a difference of quality, and say, e.g. (*Jacob* p. 84): in respect of quality they can be *affirmative* or *negative*? Did this latter statement not clearly prove to them that *their* ground is no ground at all, but merely a *conditio*, posited by them for the ground, on the side of the material, which as present *conditio* introduces the *modum ponentem*, as absent *conditio* the *modum tollentem*, into a hypothetical judgment, whereby, however, the consequence, *as consequence*, remains inalterable, and hence just as little capable of a difference of quality as of modality? Should it not at least have occurred to them, in endowing *disjunctive* judgments with the modality of the *apodictic*, that what they hold for a ground is none? For how can one set up the proposition (*Kiesewetter* p. 55) that in respect of modality disjunctive judgments are *apodictic* for this reason, *because the members determine one another reciprocally as ground and consequence*, [p. 304] in that the assumption of the one member would be the ground why one must necessarily exclude the other (or the others)? But does this mean: *the members themselves* determine one another reciprocally as ground and consequence? In this way the *learned man* would be the ground, and the unlearned man his consequence, if I said: either Caius is learned or unlearned. Yet this concerns only the confusion of language. So, to the point! I assume the case that, with regard to a certain object of the senses, one has only to choose between two colors, so that it would have to be either red or green. According to the Kantians this sole possible choice between *Two* would be the ground why one could then draw from it the *apodictic* inference and assert absolutely: the object is not yellow, not white, not black, etc. But is this presupposition, that there are only two colors that stand in the choice, then not merely an *empirical* condition, which for that very reason can by no means be a ground? And if one then asks after the real ground of that inference, apodictic under the said condition, i.e. if one wants to know the last thing, on which it necessarily rests in the world-system, [p. 305] will *this* ground not lie in something of which there is no thought in *Kant*, namely 1) in this, that through B (actuality) and (− B, possibility) everything in the world is completely determined, and that what has therefore once been brought under these two components absolutely admits of no third any more, whereby something could still be determined, (*omnimoda determinatio Logica adeoque et Physica*; *Kant*’s aggregate? *Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 112); — — 2) in this, that what is once posited in empiricism under B (= those two colors) *as posited under B* can absolutely not be taken as posited under (− B = the remaining possible colors), and what is posited under (− B), as posited under (− B), cannot be taken as posited under B; notwithstanding that (B − B) accompany one another inseparably in the world-system. It is with this as with the opposition of *honestum* and *utile*. The *honestum*, *as honestum*, is indeed no *utile*; but the *honestum* and *utile* accompany one another inseparably in a world-system, if it is to be a system, no chaos; see above, and now supplement the above scales only as follows:

[p. 306] Actuality / | / B Possibility, / | / (− B) Actuality determined through the possibility / | / (+ b) Self-interest Non-self-interest Self-interest determined through something that is no self-interest.

Self-interest, determined through something that is no self-interest, gives a third, which is neither self-interest alone, nor non-self-interest alone, but arisen from both, *human virtue*, and which inseparably joins the *utile* of the Stoics with their *honestum*; i.e. lays the ground for the only possible system of morals. But if this virtue is to come about in man: then *man*, i.e. the (− B + b), must with consciousness be raised in him to the third power, hence be (− B³ + b³). The third, which is neither self-interest alone, nor non-self-interest alone, but arisen from both, *human virtue*, has as its inseparable companion the *benefit of the part as well as of the whole*, (the *true* benefit, *utile Stoicorum*), because [p. 307] the *form* or the *essence* of things is so expressed in it that the entire individuality of man is regulated accordingly.

5) The copula of *syllogisms*, be they categorical, hypothetical or disjunctive, is absolutely necessary and without difference of modality; or, what in every possible *syllogism* is *merely* thought (hence formally and properly *logical* in the hitherto accepted sense of the word), that is absolutely necessary and without difference of modality.

*Rem.* The same holds of the object and of the *This* (B − B + b) in every syllogism; both are absolutely necessary and without difference of modality. — — *Kant* is perfectly right when he places the nature of syllogisms in this, that for the conditioned they seek the conditions, and for the conditions again higher conditions, i.e. annihilate the most material for thinking in thinking; in order finally to be led to the *conditio sine qua non* for all conditions in the *material*. But this, which the syllogism achieves herewith, is nothing further than a more *detailed development* of what already lay enfolded in every concept, *as a complexion of affine sensible marks*, [p. 308] and which the syllogism now, *according to the manner and way in which it arose and was associated* under the formula ( − B + b B ), *partly* lays asunder, and hence also lets be *partly* annihilated by thinking in thinking, until one has finally ascended to the sensible *conditio sine qua non* for the whole row of certain sensible conditions, and now the (+ b) as cause can be posited immediately in these. This whole operation, however, would not be possible if the *Unconditioned*, as *Kant* calls it, had first to be sought through this operation. No, the unconditioned of the cause (Ur-sache), of the *ground*, of the *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, *as of an absolute*, already aroused in us beforehand, *as human beings*, by the impulse of life, *makes it precisely so* that we *can*, indeed *must*, undertake that operation with the material and ascend in it from conditions to conditions. If, e.g., the *ground* did not already lie beforehand absolutely and unconditionally *in us*, how would it be at all possible that we could undertake that operation with the material itself, what should induce us to go from condition to condition in it, until we finally came to the *conditio sine qua non* for [p. 309] all conditions in the *material*? Here too, then, *Kant* has again completely inverted a thought, excellently aroused in him, in that he lets man seek the *unconditioned* and absolute, (hence his own thinking), only through *syllogisms*, whereas man could nevertheless, without having the unconditioned and absolute in himself, neither form concepts nor make syllogisms. But that the very formula which underlies the syllogisms must already underlie the concepts is easily proved, if, after the above analysis, it still needs a proof at all. In that I say: *homo*: I posit an object, hence an actuality and possibility; there I find for the (+ b), consequently for the *genus humanum* as for the *This* (*hic homo*) the *one* limit of its origin, here (under the possibility) I find a place for the predicates, e.g. for the predicate of mortality, which is a possibility in connection with an actuality (hence − B + b), otherwise it would not be a predicate. But (− B + b) I have already in the above *homo*, consequently I now have in the judgment: *homo est mortalis* [man is mortal]: the formula ( B − B + b − B + b )

[p. 310] = ( − B² + b² B ); and I understand why it is taught in the logics that the *extent* of the predicate is greater than that of the subject. But that I can bring it, in the judgment: *homo est mortalis*, to the expression of the formula ( − B² + b² B ) presupposes in me myself, insofar as I am man, a manifestation of the essence of things, hence a ( − B + b B ) lying at the ground in me myself, *as man*, and coming to consciousness, though not necessarily *as what it is*, but also merely under sheer sensible representations; and so I have thus a ( − B³ + b³ B ) already as inescapably necessary formula for the possibility of the connection of a subject with certain marks, or of certain marks with a subject, hence for the possibility of a *concept* in general in the widest sense of the word; notwithstanding that this ( − B³ + b³ B ), before I have cleared it up with what is material in my representations, will enter my consciousness merely as [p. 311] ( − B² + b² B ). Shall I elucidate the matter still more clearly by the example just given? — So be it. I posit, then, the two named objects for a thinking once more in altered expressions for one and the same thing:

*Homo*: *Non homo*; object *Mortalis*: *Non mortalis*; object,

insofar as both are necessarily represented together in one thinking, and I can think no *Homo* without a *Non homo*, no *Mortalis* without a *Non mortalis*. — If, however, I now want to bring out the proposition:

*Homo est Mortalis* [Man is mortal];

then object comes to object, and I should accordingly have (B² − B²). But in (− B) the negation is drawn to a copula, hence to a *form* (and indeed here to the form on the material): yet no form can be annihilated by the other; rather, a negation drawn to the copula posits. The (− B) therefore posits a (+ b), and from the above *Homo*: *Non homo*, as necessarily represented together in one thinking, there must therefore arise a genus, or a *This*. Instead, therefore, of the [p. 312] *Homo*: *Non homo*

in my formula as (B − B), I shall rather have to express it thus: B (− B + b), whereby (B + b) will express for me the *Homo*, as *Homo* (*secundum id, quod est fundamenti loco ponendum* [according to that which is to be put in the place of a foundation], extension, *as extension*, given under a coexistence) and at the same time in the (+ b) as *Homo* in a genus already cognized by me in it, but the (− B) the possibility, already noticed by me in it, of its being contained under a genus; hence the latter makes room for me, for the ranging of predicates under it, under a (+ b). If I now range the predicate *mortalis* under it: then by virtue of the nature of the object I already have two propositions:

*Homo est mortalis* [Man is mortal]

*Non homo est non mortalis* [Non-man is non-mortal]; (for the *Homo* gives me, by virtue of the nature of the object, a *Non-homo*, and the *mortalis* a *non mortalis*), propositions of which the one, like the other, is necessarily grounded through my formula; and which assert nothing other than 1) the necessity of a reciprocal accompaniment of the actual by the possible, and of the possible by the actual, 2) [p. 313] the necessity of an uninterrupted distinction of the actual from the possible and vice versa, notwithstanding that they accompany each other uninterruptedly. But in the second proposition:

*Non homo est non mortalis*

the special case surely arises that there merely a possibility is set up in one and the very same proposition: yet the whole formula is supposed to hold for this proposition too. Certainly; and precisely for this reason, because no possibility takes place without actuality, notwithstanding that the possibility may never be taken for the actuality and the actuality never for the possibility. The *Non homo* thus brings with it for me, as possibility, also an actuality, notwithstanding that it is not itself this actuality, the *non mortalis* brings with it for me, as possibility, also an actuality, notwithstanding that it is not itself this actuality. The actuality determined through the possibility, as it already lay in the *Homo* as a genus, the actuality determined through the possibility, as it already lay in the *mortalis* as a genus, cannot first bring along to me the *Non homo* and *Non mortalis*, for these I already had in *Homo* and *mortalis*. Through the conjunction of the *Non homo* [p. 314] with a *non mortalis* under one thinking, there must therefore only again an actuality determined through the possibility, (a cause [*Ur*-sache], + b), and in language an *aliquid, quod nec homo nec mortale est* [something which is neither man nor mortal], be posited, just as from the above *Homo*: *Non homo*, as necessarily represented together in one thinking, a (+ b), consequently a third, which was neither *Homo* (*secundum id, quod est fundamenti loco ponendum*, extension, *as extension*, given under a coexistence), nor *Non homo*, but a *Genus hominum* or a *This*, resulted. From this I learn the following: when two objects (the object applicable in two items of cognition) come together in such a way that the one (*Homo*) under its (− B *Non homo*) makes room for the other (*mortalis*) for the attachment of its (− B, *non mortalis* together with the (+ b) inseparable from it): then there arises from this once more a (+ b), a new *genus rerum*, a new *This*, an *aliquid, quod nec homo nec mortale est*; in other words: the possibility on the side of the one object united with the possibility on the side of the other produces an actuality determined through the possibility, a *substance* or *cause* [Ur-sache], which, to be sure, is indeed also a [p. 315] (+ b), a *This*, a genus, an *aliquid*, but here in the given case an *aliquid, quod nec homo nec mortale est* [something which is neither man nor mortal], will be. Through the proposition: *Non homo est non mortalis*, an *aliud quid quod nec homo nec mortale est* has thus become actual; but it has become actual under a possibility; will therefore also be merely actual and *will have to* be actual, insofar as the actuality is accompanied there by the possibility, just as it can only be possible insofar as the possibility is accompanied there by the actuality; for, as to the latter, the (− B), without a (+ b) posited thereby, would be annihilation of a form, hence a contradiction. If in this way the *aliud quid, quod nec homo nec mortale est*, has arisen merely through thinking, *as a thinking* and the object, *as object*, and must it therefore be thought with its necessity: then in the given example the necessary *aliud quid*, insofar as it *here* points to an imperishability, leads the *thinking* man necessarily beyond sensibility, the sensible, on the other hand, it will cause to realize *its aliud quid* in the given example under phantasy-images, hence to commit a contradiction *insofar as he* [p. 316] seeks the actuality, *as actuality*, in the *Kantian* manner, in the world of sense or in something intuitable. I take yet another concept, *as object*, before I go further, and see what follows. Let it be the concept of the *world*:

*Mundus*: *Non mundus*; object,

*Mundus* thus expresses the *actuality* (B), *Non mundus* the possibility (− B) in the object. The actuality can indeed, as actuality, never be taken for the possibility, hence *Mundus* never for *Non mundus*, but the actuality is just as certainly accompanied, everywhere and inseparably, by the possibility, *hence the world by something that is not world*, as the possibility by the actuality; and here I am now with my (− B) again up at that which, for its *being* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, needs no more any possibility nor actuality *of the kind that that of the object is*, at *A as A, in A, through A*, with you, then, Father and Lord of the world, all-enlivening, beneficent Spirit, who created human beings after your image, and in whose contemplation my soul wells up with rapture! I descend again from you, Eternal One, — to [p. 317] man, as I weighed him with his sensible mortality, and posit him once more as object:

*Homo*: *Non homo*; object.

Through the *Non homo* room is made in our representation for an unsurveyable multitude of other possible things besides man; consequently thereby not only apprehensions, *as experiences*, but also apprehensions as mere configurations of coexistence, of opposition, of similarity *in our phantasy*, an immeasurable field is opened. *Experiences* they will be, if the (B − B + b), aroused in us by certain apprehensions, has so annihilated the material in these apprehensions under one thinking down to (B − B + b) or down to the form thereon, that now no longer merely the apprehension (or the material) may arouse the rule in us, but the rule determines the material as it is determined outside us under the very same rule, if consequently the object in us as a *This* (we ourselves) an object outside us as a *This*, through the object as a *This*,

( − B³ + b³ B ), under one thinking necessarily [p. 318] determines. These are experiences, or proper concepts, complexions of sensible marks in their necessary relation first to the cause (Ur-sache) as cause, then to the ground, and finally to the *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν; they are not merely inductively registered observations of certain conditions in the material, but observations which now stand in us under the very same necessary rule under which they stand in the world-system itself. If here the form, the rule or the essence of the universal (of the whole) were not also form, rule, essence of the particular (of the part): *Copernicus* and *Kepler* could have had their rules for the true world-system long in their heads, without the world-system ever having conformed to them in the experiences of later astronomers (e.g. a *Herschel*’s), unless *Copernicus* and *Kepler* had been able, like *Fichte*, to draw the world-system straight out of their I, and *Herschel* and every astronomer of later centuries enjoyed the same good fortune of an I creating suns and planets in fairest order out of itself. So long as in apprehensions and observations concerning certain objects the material indeed arouses [p. 319] the rule in us, but the rule does not yet determine the material as necessarily as it is determined outside us under the same rule, so long we have no proper experiences concerning such objects, but what has been vouchsafed to us about them are opinions (δόξαι), where there is still always a *Yes* or *No*, are confusions of conditions with the cause (Ur-sache). The elucidation of what the language of life, and what *Kant*, calls experience, hence *the origin of a human cognition in general*, (under the new title of a critique), can now in any case present no further difficulty:

*Conditions of the Possibility of an Experience.*

1) A *thinking* (*A as A in A through A*)

2) Needful *hypothesis* (substratum); *coexistence in the imagination.* (Material, which affects thinking in a succession and juxtaposition)

[p. 320] 3) Disjunction of the coexisting in the *imagination.* (exclusive *either—or*, holding up of the opposite of that which is there, consequently of the possibility without actuality, consequently of an impossibility through the law of opposition in the *imagination*).

*Rem.* If this sounds too harsh to anyone, let him consider whether the presence of a deceased friend is possible, and yet he is present to him in his phantasy, hence his phantasy entertains him in this case with an impossibility; and did it not entertain him with such impossibilities, then no affinities, no complexions of sensible marks, no concepts could contract in him. Yes, still more: how should we human beings even manage to attain cognition and application of the principle of contradiction, if our phantasy were not capable of presenting impossibilities to us, over which the thinking applied to them can then first pronounce the *being-impossible*?

[p. 321] 4) A compatibility, brought about through coexistence and disjunction in the *imagination*, of that which in the material itself agrees together, namely of the *affinities*.

5) Disjunction in the *object*; the form rises above the material, in order to let the material, *as material*, join itself under (B − B + b), hence under form, with the character of regularity and persistence, to this (B − B + b), (*unbound* monad in alteration under a rule). (non-exclusive *either—or*, B − B inseparably together, and necessarily producing a + b).

6) A real union (causal connection, *causatio reciproca*), brought about through the disjunction in the *object*, of that which in the material itself agrees together, namely of the *affinities*, under (B − B + b).

7) Where now the action of the (A as A in A through A) on a certain material does not eradicate this material so far that it could come to the coexistence of the present with the past, of the near [p. 322] with the far, etc. in an (individual) imagination, hence to oppositions in an (individual) imagination, and thereby to the summing-up of the similarities in an (individual) imagination, in short to an (individual) *animal* becoming-conscious, that it consequently remains merely at the union of the affinities in the material under a (B − B + b) *after preceding attraction and repulsion*, there arises also merely a *plant life*, (*slumbering* monad).

8) Where, on the other hand, the action of the (A as A in A through A) on a certain material eradicates this material so far that *sense-organs, as sense-organs*, can arise, hence an *individual* consciousness becomes possible, there then also an individual imagination or imagining-into the world and of the world into such a world-being, hence, after the preceding process of Nos. 3–6 incl., a regular running-off of the world *outside him* (in his *representation*), as *in him* (in his *sensation*), hence a ( − B² + b² B ) will take place; *animal* life, (*dreaming* monad).

[p. 323] 9) But a regular running-off of the world, in itself, *outside him* (in his *representation*) as *in him* (in his *sensation*), does not yet necessarily imply that such a world-being must also have insight into the *possibility* in the rule according to which both run off. Notwithstanding, therefore, that his essence consists of ( − B² + b² B ), the formula of his essence can nevertheless enter his consciousness merely as ( + b² B ), and *will have to* enter his consciousness merely as this, if the (*A as A in A through A*) does not also eradicate the material brought by the imagination to such a being (in representations), *in these representations*, down to the form of beings in general, hence not down to the actuality *determined through the possibility, as possibility*, and consequently wants to know the same led to possibility only under *sensations as mere sensations*, (instinct), to actuality only under representations, *as mere representations*, according to the plan of eternal wisdom; (*animal, as dreaming* monad).

10) But the case posited under No. 9 can just as well be reversed. There can [p. 324] just as well also be world-beings who are to be led to possibility not only under *sensations as mere sensations*, to actuality not only under *representations, as mere representations*, according to the plan of eternal wisdom; but in whom, besides the gift of a merely animal becoming-conscious, already according to their essence, *actuality also still as actuality is to be determined through possibility also still as through possibility* (concept, experience), in whom therefore to the ( − B² + b² B ) of an animal being there still comes a ( − B + b B ) which will declare the *peculiarity* of their essence in a ( − B³ + b³ B ); (*waking* monad, *man in his essence*: at the same time the highest end of matter, that under God’s guidance it becomes a nursery of spirits).

11) What was said under No. 10 will come to pass when the (*A as A, in A, through A*) does not, in the material of such beings, eradicate the material merely to the union of the affinities under a (B − B + b) after preceding attraction and repulsion [p. 325] (plant life *in us*), and not merely down to the coexistence of the present with the past, of the near with the far in an (individual) imagination, hence to oppositions in an (individual) imagination, and thereby to the summing-up of the similarities in an (individual) imagination (individual *animal* becoming-conscious *in us*); but there, where in any case a minus of the becoming-conscious of the *material, as material*, had to enter, merely in order to make an individual consciousness possible, in the life-impulse, *as impulse in general*, it so annihilates also completely everything that is not essence, this indestructible impulse, *as impulse*, excepted, that now the *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν itself, the (*A as A, in A, through A*) with *its* plus, can enter consciousness completely, and for such world-beings the full determinability of the essence of things through the essence of the same, in a now attained *personality* (in the object as a now possible object of their consciousness itself), hence in a ( − B³ + b³ B ), as *characteristic* ingredient of their essence, can become clear.

[p. 326] 12) If this ( − B³ + b³ B ) is once a *characteristic* ingredient of the essence of a certain class of beings itself: then again three cases are possible. Either the object *with the attached life-impulse* (the − B + b B, as manifestation of the essence of things *in and on us*), in short our personality, takes a certain judgment, which would otherwise consist only of ( − B² + b² B ), up *into itself*, and thereby makes it *for us* a ( − B³ + b³ B ), *we vouch for a thing*; or a judgment is determined through the object cognized in a thing *outside us*, and thereby becomes for *everyone* a ( − B³ + b³ B ); or else neither of the two named cases occurs, neither does our personality, *as personality*, take a certain judgment up *into itself*, nor is the judgment determined through the object cognized in a thing *outside us*, and it therefore remains with this merely at determinations of actuality through possibility under sheer representations and sensations, consequently at the ( − B² + b² B ), [p. 327] although with expressly preceding (− B²) in consciousness.

13) In the first case (the taking up of a judgment into our personality *as personality*), α) *we, for our part*, are undeniably convinced of something, assert and assure it as *our* indisputable experience, (*subjective* necessity in a *special* experience in general); or else β) we make ourselves in such a judgment answerable for a thing, not only take on but also assure to others the obligation to a performance determined therein on our side, (*subjective* necessity in an obligation, *obligatio*), in that we connect the same with our personality, *as personality*, by means of the ( − B³ + b³ B ).

A contract, e.g., in which the given conditions of the possibility of a cognition, from those of the imagination onward, (if one posits there as affinities merely the *convenience* of the paciscents) are completely justified, a *contract* thus expresses nothing other than the connection of the judgment about certain objects with the personality of two human beings *as* [p. 328] *personality* by means of the ( − B³ + b³ B ), which *here* in the third power has taken the object *with the attached life-impulse* into itself.

14) In the second case (of the determining of a judgment through the object cognized in a thing *outside us*) there arise *universally-necessary* truths, *mathematically evident and demonstrative* propositions, hence experiences, such as those are which the astronomers can *now* make about the motions of the heavenly bodies, after *Copernicus and Kepler* once determined them as object, and, independently of mere intuitions, realized in them the formula of the essences, which *here* in the third power has taken the object in a thing *outside us* into itself, and now everything that belongs to this thing lets itself be attached demonstratively, in itself, *as its rule*.

*Rem.* Even the observations of the empiricist offer themselves for the elucidation of Nos. 13, 14. Our personality steps out of consciousness in those efforts of *thinking, as a thinking*, which promote the health of the spirit more than anything else, [p. 329] in order to determine something beyond its sensible exterior *as object*. If, on the other hand, with a judgment that would in itself be merely ( − B² + b² B ), we connect also our personality, *as personality*, and *thereby* produce a ( − B³ + b³ B ) in consciousness: then it is not possible, in those *same* moments, that this judgment could at the same time also take into itself (demonstratively) the object in a thing *outside us* in consciousness, as may be clear already from this, that in that case it cannot well go on without special participation of our *individuality* in the thing and without emotions. How calm, by contrast, is the mathematician when, chaining syllogisms to syllogisms, he passes over the material to actuality (B, *extension as extension*), determined through possibility (− B, *reckoning*).

15) In the third case (posited under No. 12), where neither our personality, *as personality*, has taken up a certain judgment into itself, nor a judgment determined through the object, cognized in a thing *outside us*, [p. 330] has been, there arises a mere *perhaps*, a probability, a holding-for-true *non sine formidine oppositi* [not without fear of the opposite], a dream, although with preceding (− B²) in consciousness.

But I forget that I wanted to write merely an outline, and to conclude it with modality.
