The copula of every possible syllogism is absolutely necessary, apodictic and without difference of modality, it was said above. *Kiesewetter* (l. c. p. 108) remarks of the modality of a *pure categorical syllogism*, (as he expresses himself in § 232), that, according to No. 8 ibid. “*the conclusion of the same receives the lowest degree of the modality occurring in the premises, according to No. 9 ibid., however, the conclusion is nevertheless necessary.*” Thus the lowest degree of the modality occurring in the premises would fall to the conclusion, and yet the conclusion would be necessary. One is tempted to take this latest Logical Sidiroxylon once again for a misprint, but finds, on closer consideration of the two rules, under § 232, that Nos. 8 and 9 really concern the *modality* of a pure categorical syllogism, whose conclusion would accordingly, beside the fact [p. 331] that it, as conclusion, is necessary and apodictic, would also still, as conclusion from *certain* premises, be problematic or assertoric. I know no other way, this time, to explain to myself this exemplary guidance of the most celebrated new logic to downright contradictions, than that its author, with the transference of the lowest degree of the modality occurring in the premises to the conclusion (nonetheless apodictic and necessary in its modality), wanted to modernize the old Logical Canon *Kantianly*: *conclusio sequitur partem debiliorem* [the conclusion follows the weaker part], i.e. the quality (but not modality) of the conclusion is governed by the quality of that premise which has the lowest (a negative) quality, and the quantity (not however modality) of the conclusion is governed by the quantity of that premise which has the lowest (a particular) quantity.

[p. 332]

## Conclusion.

If *A, as A, in A, through A*, acts on C, there arises 1) B a *something* thought (an *extension, as extension*, without further possible alteration of the extended, an *actuality*) and 2) (− B) a *what is thought, as what is thought*, (*possibility*, intention, end). From this something thought and from what is thought, *as what is thought*, there then springs, as from the *ground* (B) and the possibility (− B) under a *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, a (+ b), in that a negation drawn to the copula cannot annihilate the copula, *as form*, but rather posits it, and indeed, if it is the copula of the material, hence space with its three dimensions, posits it under multiplications of these three indestructible dimensions of space in and under themselves; (to let the copula, hence a *form*, be annihilated by a negation drawn to the copula itself, would be a contradiction). This form of the whole now ( − B + b B ) must also be form of the part, every one of my judgments tells me this, which, without that presupposition, as judgment, (as connection of a thing already *in its essence* irreconcilable) would not at all be possible [p. 333] and this the world-system tells me, which, without that presupposition, as *system* (as unity of a thing already *in its essence* irreconcilable) would not at all be possible, etc. But what is most important here is that that formula points back, undeniably and with apodictic irrefutability, to a being of beings, if no contradiction is to be committed, and the *system* of things is not to be begun with a minus (− B). There must be a *God* who, *as God*, is all that he is only *in and through* himself. In the conscience of man he reveals himself; this was recognized, awakened by an inner voice, even by the most resolute unbelief itself, often enough. In the thinking of man in general, *as a thinking*, in every identity that leads me absolutely beyond the material, *as mere material*, hence in every rule, hence in the whole world-order as order, whose possibility without a thinking wise being would be an impossibility, this God reveals himself, I now know this, and rejoice in it, with apodictic irrefutability. It would be audacious if, with what he has revealed to me of himself in my thinking, I believed I had *fathomed* his essence in general, and I therefore call that which he has, in my thinking, of himself [p. 334] revealed to me, merely the indestructible *modus* of a being κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, which I *must* assume besides this changeable existence if, considering my thinking itself and in and for itself, I do not wish to make it into a *dialexis*, i.e. to make my reason into unreason. If I follow the law of identity, the rule of all rules in me, if I take what lies before me sensibly into a thinking, not merely into an association: then I follow truth, duty and right. The essence and order of things, truth, duty and right, must therefore proceed from something whose being, precisely because it is removed from the sensible conditions of the material, can be placed under the condition of a possible intuition only through a contradiction, cannot be expressed more correctly than through the being of an *ens realissimum*, of an *ens entium*, of a pure intelligence, or of a *spirit*, of a *spiritual* substance. Let someone tell me a way that remained still open, in the shattered circumstances of our sickly German philosophy, to lay hold again of that jewel of humanity, if it was not the way of a consistent analysis of our thinking. I took it without this intention; this I confess myself. Errors in the logics had struck me, and only these I should gladly have got to the [p. 335] bottom of. That this undertaking has now disclosed to me—or given back—truths of which, as propositions *capable of such evidence*, I had not had the slightest inkling a quarter of a year ago: this is the sweetest reward, which no applause can give me, no blame take from me.—I will conclude, and must therefore take my leave also of the *Kantian* philosophy. Beyond the limits of a possible intuition, reason contrives merely by means of *wretched* syllogisms (*Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 283) in a subjective semblance an objective reality (l. c. p. 396–399) which it then lays as the ground of a supposed supersensible science, called *metaphysics*; this is the result of this philosophy in its critiquing of pure reason. A possible intuition would accordingly have the reason, critiqued in this way, behind it in the case posited by *Kant* himself; it would be beyond the limits of the same. Notwithstanding, however, that in the case posited by himself he assumes it removed beyond the limits of even an *only possible* intuition, he nonetheless reproaches it that what it still wants to know beyond has no longer any objective, i.e. no intuitive reality presentable in any object of sense, and so demands [p. 336] something of it which, according to his own presupposition, according to which he lets it already be beyond all *even only possible* intuition, can reasonably no longer be demanded of it, namely the corroboration of its syllogisms by the substitution of objects *palpable to the senses*. Is that not a sophistry like the chicanery of a *Gorgias*? *Kant*, it seems at least, wanted to make earnest of the venerable *Mendelssohn’s* jest: *I am playing out of my pocket*; (in order to explain this profane assertion to oneself the better, compare with it, in the *Kantian* philosophy, whatever you please, and see whether every fundamental thought of it, even if it is rightly grasped here and there, is not instantly twisted again; but chiefly be pleased to consult for this purpose his *Metaphysical First Principles of the Doctrine of Right*, where among other masterpieces in the art this too shines, that, despite his resolution of the good into the unchangeable laws of reason, which preceded in the *Critique of Practical Reason* and is in itself unobjectionable, he can now nonetheless, likewise *a priori*, hence likewise *according to necessary laws of thinking itself*, let it be now lawful what is wrong, now wrong what is lawful, pp. 139, 140, according [p. 337] as one and the same case at law is brought before the private-legal-practical, or before the court-legal-practical, but each time *pure* reason.). But no, I just recollect myself; it is sheer misunderstandings that divide us, and these misunderstandings rest merely once more on a certain kind of unpardonable πρῶτον ψεῦδος, such as a critical reviewer of the first magnitude long ago once proved one to me in my own work directly out of the *Critique*, as I still well know, and as will remain unforgettable to me. That *objective* reality, concerning which reason, beyond the limits of experience, is called to account by its judge and can no longer give account and answer, is anything but objective in the common sense, but objective in a new, deeper sense of the word. There is nothing spatiotemporal or palpable to the senses left in the objective; *and yet it is objective*. For, although in the *Critique of Pure Reason* an *objective* reality is denied to what lies beyond the limits of intuition: yet in the *Critique of Practical Reason* (p. 207 etc., where, however, the passage from *Epicurus’s Canonic*, p. 217, might likewise be noted) and in the *Metaphysical First Principles of the Doctrine of Right* [p. 338] (p. 141 etc.) the *objective* is taken also in such a way that it must designate a thing in itself, and not merely something lying in the subjective representation of man. Objective is, as one needs it, now sensibly-, now thingly-objective; whoever means it otherwise understands only the trade of *right and left at once*, not yet *comme il faut*. Beyond the limits of a possible intuition, theoretical reason can merely posit nothing *thingly-objective* any more without lying; for of the *sensibly- or intuitively-objective* one may, to be sure, by virtue of the self-same presupposition, reasonably no longer speak here; this would mean as much as to ask whether one can still have or make intuitions where one had oneself previously declared intuitions impossible. So it is to be taken; whoever takes it otherwise knows nothing. But why, I permit myself to ask, can theoretical reason accordingly posit nothing *thingly-objective* any more beyond the limits of a possible intuition? and I receive from the Critique the answer: because I tell you (*per petitionem principii*) that my categories become blind beyond the limits of a possible intuition. Intuition, I must therefore judge, it is consequently after all, which also [p. 339] must found the *thingly-objective* as reality. Where no intuition is possible any more, there is hence no reality any more; rather everything is to be rejected as empty ratiocination which (in the domain of *speculative* reason) cannot vouch for its objective reality through evident examples to be set up in experience, (before it proceeds *practically*, *Critique of Practical Reason*, p. 217), and the proposition remains unshakably standing: *Kant has demanded of speculative reason still intuitions as the only possible tests of reality in its assertions, after he had himself beforehand removed it beyond the limits of the possibility of any intuition whatever, and insofar as he had himself beforehand removed it beyond them; has thus imposed on it a performance, to meet which, through his own self-same presupposition, under which the performance was to take place, had been made impossible by him in his own person, and has then condemned the highest that humanity possesses, considered as something merely in and for itself, on that account, because it cannot perform what he nonetheless through his* [p. 340] *own presupposition, under this presupposition to perform, himself and in his own person, had made impossible for it to perform, has however notwithstanding this asserted* before as after once more, his only possible tests of reality, the intuitions of pure as well as impure nature, *lead* nevertheless *to no reality, to no thing in itself*, but to mere appearances, mathematics itself is indefeasible only for the sake of the intuitions, not for the sake of the (without intuitions wretched) syllogisms on which it rests, has thus let mathematics too attain to no reality in itself, but has degraded it, unprecedentedly, only to a necessary appearance; metaphysics, on the other hand, as a science at whose ground lie syllogisms without intuition, *he first rejected entirely*, and afterwards nonetheless built two of its principal pieces up again on a *fact, as fact*, hence in accordance with his own system built on a necessary appearance (for we do not, as is well known, get to things in themselves), and indeed on a necessary appearance *in thinking itself, as a thinking*? which lets itself also be coarsely sensualized through contingent appearances, through acts, and, *because it lets itself be coarsely sensualized*, lets [p. 341] also to that which they presuppose for their possibility (which, then, *as ground of possibility*, could surely by no means be intuitable?), likewise objective, i.e. intuitable, reality be assured. This sensibly palpable, gross reality (which, however, is no reality either, for we do not, as is well known, get to things in themselves) would now be that of which all philosophy before *Kant* in its simplicity never thought, would be e.g. not a merely *subjective* representation of God, but rather the proclamation of an objective, intuitable, palpable existence in him, although the idea of him, on account of its lack of intuitability, had previously been declared dialectical, i.e. something supported on mere wretched syllogisms and categories gone blind, hence senseless and meaningless, yet had at the same time, as *something senseless and meaningless*, been stamped a *regulative* for the whole of our cognitions of experience? Alongside this and in the same critical spirit the possibility of thinking is then grounded only through concepts (categories (*Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 125 ff. and concepts of reflection), the possibility of syllogizing only through judgments, although it is *thinking as thinking* that first makes concepts possible, or *reason as reason* that first makes judgments as well as syllogisms possible. Alongside this and in the same [p. 342] critical spirit thinking, as an unconditioned and as an *absolute* possibility which strays beyond the form of thinking of this name, (l. c. p. 285), must itself seek itself only in syllogisms; whereas, in order to arrive at the possibility of an intuition, one simply commands man to intuit already *a priori* in space and time, and makes him an apriorist visionary, who finally grows into a world-head that embraces in visions everything that is called reality, but which, to be sure, can communicate its private apriorist gift of vision (called *one and pure* philosophy) to no man any more. Alongside this, and regardless that the categories present the function of the unity of thinking, l. c. p. 94, the category itself nevertheless already presupposes combination, p. 131; this combination, however, is not the analytic form of thinking itself, (that would be merely *logical*, not *critical*, p. 267), but the *originally synthetic*? unity of apperception, hence the *animal* consciousness with the twelve forms of thinking already analyzed by it, from which the: *I think*: results (pp. 131–143). Alongside this, and regardless that a law to which no intuition were added would have to be not real (or only half real?), it is (*Critique of Practical Reason*, p. 68) declared for a [p. 343] flat contradiction, “to think to oneself a feeling (hence surely also an intuition) of a *law as such, to think, and to make that the object of sensation which can only be thought through reason.*” Are these series of contradictions not just so many philosophical cancers; and could one heal cancers with rose water?—Let there come of it for him what may, the honest man speaks freely from the heart where truth is concerned, in the face of the triumphant scornful laughter of hollow chicaneries? At the *laughing of uncritical ignoramuses* the Critique seems anyway to have been aimed; for thus speaks its author (p. X, cf. p. IX of the Preface to the *Metaphysical First Principles of the Doctrine of Right*) “if it is a test of the truth of a doctrine not to be despised, when it endures *laughter*: then in time it must surely be the critical philosophers’ turn to laugh *last, and so also best*.” Whoever has shown so intuitively through his own example that the *paper systems* (ibid.) of a metaphysics can be put an end to only through contradictions upon contradictions, hence only through an ending of thinking itself, hence only through a critique below all critique, [p. 344] to him it is to be granted, if he at least still *laughs best*; he has not philosophized best. But how is it to be explained that it was possible for a philosopher already *in primis viis* [at the very first steps], by means of a complete setting aside of the principle of contradiction, indeed by means of a genial skill, through the law of contrast in his imagination, of combining precisely the most contrasting most easily, (*Hogarthian* manner in philosophy), thus to proceed, and yet withal with many hundred pages of flat contradictions to succeed so exceedingly with his public? This question is answered by the age into which he had the luck to fall. It is the age of milking the he-goat, (*words of the Critique of Pure Reason*, pp. 82, 83), whereby, because it is something new, thousands come, and hold the sieve. Suffice it for me to restrict this to philosophy. *Leibniz* had in vain opposed the attempts of *Locke*, to explain everything about man from experience; his deep view was only for few among his own people; for foreigners, it seems, not at all. *Britons and Frenchmen* persisted with *Locke*, (see the *Discours préliminaire* to the French *Encyclopédie*). In trust in the invincibility of the *Lockean* principles and under the most confident expectations [p. 345] of the real thanks which their idol, sensibility and imagination, would render them for it, there came about at last in both nations a *mechanical* mode of explanation of the origin of the whole of human cognition, a psychological brain-fibre system, the form derived from the oscillating material, thinking from (hypothetical) intuitabilities, the analysis of thinking itself from a synthesis of the manifold, the essence from the non-essence, and hence, as the *Critique of Pure Reason* says, in philosophy, (*at the very least*), the business of milking the he-goat introduced as the future order of the day for the century of enlightenment. Of those who came and held the sieve there were countless, and among these countless, as goes without saying, (*car même ce Ms. Leibniz n’étoit qu’un Allemand* [for even this Mr Leibniz was only a German]), the Germans too, and among the Germans—*Kant*. With *his own manipulations* this man set about the work. He understood, like no other, *the right and the left at once*; *Leibniz* and *Locke* had to be reconciled: the *Critique of Pure Reason* came to be (*per Syncretismum enormem* [through a monstrous syncretism]). *Leibniz* had, what he knew, in *Kant’s* eyes known only by halves; the Briton *Locke* fared no better, (*Critique of Pure Reason*, pp. 118, 119:) whoever [p. 346] was able to play both halves together, *only he* obtained a whole piece. The oscillating brain-fibres were thus scraped and polished. Scraped and polished, they acquired a *Leibnizian* mien, passed over into apriorities, and gave, net, a set of twelve dishes under the foreign name of the *categories*, whose originator (the Greek *Aristotle*) can surely never have dreamt that his *modi enunciationis* [modes of enunciation] (σχήματα κατηγορίας, *de Decem Categor.* Cap. IV) would one day be misused *for possibilities of a thinking in general*. But the short-sighted *Leibniz* (also one of the uncritical ignoramuses, l. c.) had not noticed that the apriority of the understanding, which he took under his protection, is blind by nature, that it consists only in a *half* reality, the other half of whose reality it expects only from England, from *Locke’s* intuitions; this (that sensibility must realize the understanding, *Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 187) was noticed only at the end of the enlightened century, and indeed even then only in *Königsberg* alone. Hardly had this been noticed, acutely beyond compare: *tum* [then]—

[p. 347] *Vidimus flavum Tiberim, retortis* *Littore Etrusco violenter undis,*

*Ire dejectum monumenta regis* *Templaque Vestæ.* [We have seen the yellow Tiber, its waves violently driven back from the Etruscan shore, go to cast down the monuments of the king and the temples of Vesta.] It was now done with metaphysics, which only an inconsistency in *Locke* (*Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 127) could still save, for ever and ever. Stretched out ell-long in twelve categories and four pairs of concepts of reflection, the human understanding now lay there, and lay senseless and meaningless, hence incapable of any future metaphysics, there now, until the beneficent fairy of a *pure* intuition (which is of purely *Kantian* origin, Preface to the *Metaphysical First Principles of the Doctrine of Right*, pp. VII, VIII) took pity on him, insofar as he renounced the bungling of a *metaphysics*, brought him, lower down, the half of a foreign reality he had missed higher up, let him intuit *a priori* (without eyes), and had what he had intuited *a priori* finally sealed, grossly, *a posteriori*; *nam, quid juvat aspectus si non conceditur usus?* [for what good is sight, if its use is not granted?] p. 195, *Critique of Pure Reason*.—If in this way empirical psychology (whose epoch began with *Locke*), in its doctrine of the *senses*, was so distorted that it was now called *transcendental*:

[p. 348] just as an original common disposition must lie at the basis of the senses of all men, so that in so many thousand human heads they accord with one another about colors, tones etc., (*Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 63) so there must also lie at the basis of the senses of all men an original (apriorist), but as *transcendental* far differing from that? space- and time-disposition (entitled pure intuition), so that they accord with *Kant’s* philosophy: thus empirical psychology too was, in its doctrine of *phantasy*, nothing less than spared *becoming-transcendental*. For one had, namely, besides the understanding already finished off, now still one’s dear trouble with *pure reason*, in order to be able to give metaphysics the well-deserved dust-broom, *in all due legal form*. In the doctrine of phantasy the empirical psychologist now taught: beyond the limits of a possible apprehension the phantasy-images have no objective reality, inasmuch as no actual object in intuition can be laid under them there any more. More welcome he could have taught nothing for a critique in which, with pure reason itself, beyond the limits of a possible apprehension, it was not [p. 349] any longer supposed to be right. For the word *phantasy* this critique now needed merely to substitute the expression *pure reason*, and to pass off that which it had drawn over from phantasy (*per heteronomiam* [through heteronomy]) into the essence of thinking as a *transcendental* insight, possible only to a critique, hitherto never experienced, into the exposed places of pure reason itself, and an age accustomed to nothing so much as to empiricism believed that quite gladly also, and entirely, of its own pure reason, of which it found daily proofs in its phantasy. The believing, to which *Kant*, as he says, first had to make room? followed in this regard all the more philosophically, since indeed even he who had still learned logic could develop of itself *this* result of a new philosophy already from the old proposition of his logic, that the physically true is indeed also logically true, but the logically true, on the other hand, need not be so also physically true, through an uncommonly easy consequence, without so many extravagances; for if the logically true (possible) need not be so also physically true (actual), *because it is once and for all logically true*, it follows that for logical truth apprehension (as an integrating part of its reality?) is unavoidably required. Also the pertness [p. 350] of the discipline-forgetting pure reason had thus now found its master completely in this way in *Kant*. He, namely, as her supposedly best friend, coaxed from her that hitherto secret of her phantasy-nature down to the very bottom, (thus one speaks as *newest critic*); but was also incautious enough to prattle forthwith before all the world what she had revealed to him alone and in confidence. According to authentic reports of the Critique it ran: “she, pure reason, did not wish to withhold from him how it fared with her utterances no better than with her sister phantasy. Beyond the limits of a possible experience she deceived the people, like that one, with an empty subjective semblance; was innocent, however, in doing so: for she had to do it, and could indeed do no otherwise as *pure reason*. Only in that which she confided to her *Kant* about herself beyond the limits of a possible experience she surely did not lie. To be sure, it was in this way all over not only with all metaphysics, but also with all logic, as science, indeed even, if one looks at it in the light, with all morals, and it would seem to the uninitiated in general that her first chosen one lacked, with his excellent genius, *everywhere at the foundations* [p. 351] But if *He* it be who thinks of saving some small pieces of the science of metaphysics, and of the two others just named everything hitherto belonging to them, or also of enriching the same with new discoveries: then he need only *postulate*, and it will come to pass. From now on everything is possible to him; speaking against will present as little difficulty to him, as formerly with certain famous men in Greece, as speaking for (*Critique of Pure Reason*, p. 697, cf. pp. 353, 355, 369–399, 170, 171 etc.). But he must not betray her; for otherwise whoever seeks wisdom might learn either merely how one, after the table of categories has been abolished and pure intuitions have departed, maltreats her, reason, herself; indeed how one must give objective trouble to eternal wisdom, or he would want, for his part, to postulate something again, to have his *own* pure intuitions again, and then things would finally arise from them that would not be on a par with the time. Only to him, then, is the secret entrusted *sub sigillo silentii reciproci* [under the seal of reciprocal silence]; herewith she dismisses him, and remains etc.” Since now, however, *Kant*, as said, did not keep his word; but put pure reason, with constant reference to her *own* declaration [p. 352] made to him, on trial publicly, hence through herself: thus we all in Germany learned on this occasion that pure reason could and must, *as pure*, hence quite and entirely out of herself, like a phantasizer, create an *ideal*, that pure reason could and must, *as pure*, hence quite and entirely out of herself, like a phantasizer, fall into a *paralogism* and into an *antinomy*, i.e. into a conflict of her own laws among themselves, that thinking must therefore abolish itself, *as a thinking*, of necessity, and destroy itself according to its ground and essence, *as pure thinking*.—

The wheel of creation stands still; the night of chaos breaks in again:—*reason begins to rave, and must rave, insofar as she is pure reason*. Critics, are those not cancers?—Fare you well; I return to the Greeks. Logic itself shall follow in its time.

[p. 353]

## Supplement.

An Unknown, to whom the advance sheet B of this work came into view, found, as I hear, the *Test on the 12th §* p. 21, not intelligible enough. For the benefit of such as might fare just so, I therefore add here this.—If one seeks to put something thought into the state of the not-thought: then each time again something thought comes of it, or else it was never something thought: *cogitatum nunquam potest esse s. fieri non cogitatum* [what is thought can never be or become not thought]. Precisely for this reason the copula too, *as copula*, can never be negated, i.e. never be made a non-copula. If e.g. in *doctus* or *omnis* thinking, *as a thinking*, is once posited, and in the series of the infinitely repeated repeatability of A as A in *doctus* or *omnis* a Non A is held up to it by the imagination: then in thinking itself, *as a thinking*, this Non A becomes each time again a non Non-A = A. Why? Because no copula, as copula, can be negated without its legitimating itself, through the negating attempted on it, as indestructible and unalterable, insofar as it is copula, i.e. *form*. Suppose I set in my imagination: *doctus non est doctus*, [p. 354] or, *omnis non est omnis*, together, and thus draw here, in a mere representation for my thinking, the negation to the copula (of thinking itself): then there must come of it in my thinking, *as a thinking*: *doctus non est Non-doctus* (A = A); *omnis non est Non-omnis* (A = A); *prohibiting* imperative. *Cogitatum, qua cogitatum, semper unum idemque est* (ἔστι), *perceptum, qua perceptum, non semper unum idemque manet* (ῥέει) [what is thought, as thought, is always one and the same; what is perceived, as perceived, does not always remain one and the same]. A complete confusion of elementary concepts must therefore be presupposed in a head which denies to the thought *as a thought*, under the name of a mere (human) *subjectivity*, the reality of *being*; for to take up thinking, as a thinking, and the thought, as a thought, even only into the concept of a mere (human) subjectivity, as into their ground depending on this subjectivity or determined through it, and on that account to want to impute to thinking as well as to the thought partly a merely subjectively imagined (dialectical), partly however a reality first to be brought about through intuitions, already betrays a *vitium primae concoctionis* [fault of the first digestion] (see above). The thought, *as thought, is*, and is without all regard to human subjectivity, which [p. 355] can give it, as a thought, nothing of reality, and take nothing from it. An essence of all essences, e.g., a *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, a God, is a thought, *as thought*; to an essence of all essences, to a *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, to a God, there *must* accordingly also pertain a *being*, without all regard to human subjectivity, which can give it, as a thought, nothing of reality and take nothing from it.—Should the (B − B) also find difficulties; “because indeed, as everyone already knows from arithmetic, B is *obviously* cancelled again by a (− B):” so serves as answer. 1) *Here* (in B − B, *as the only possible* description of the object, see above) the minus is applied by a form (through thinking) to another form (to that of the material), and no form can annihilate the other; but manifests itself merely in the other form as a minus (a negation) of that which this other form is, from which manifestation (of a minus of that which it is in it, what it itself is), then just this other form in a (+ b) too actually quite unharmed (but under purposes) emerges. 2) Every negation is a positing of that which is negated, *outside that* from which or on which it is negated; this the mathematician too must [p. 356] admit. But in admitting this, he admits at the same time that in every negation α) a something positing it indeed as negation, but itself and in itself not-negative (a thinking), that β) in every negation a something taking it up indeed as negation, but itself and in itself not-negative (inalterable externality), is presupposed, whereby then the negations of the *here or there*, hence the positings of that which is negated, *outside that* from which or on which it is negated (*negationes in concreto*), are first made possible. That, however, which first makes all these negations *in concreto* possible, the minus namely in the *object* (in − B), can surely not stand without necessary and inseparable connection with that which is negated in it (notwithstanding that this minus is nothing less than the negated itself); otherwise it would be negated into infinity, and *no* negation would have sense and meaning: the zero itself would stand at the top as ground of the possibility of all negating. Who can think this? And if one cannot, one thereby admits that (B − B) *in the meaning of the object fixed here* is by no means = 0, but rather = (B − B + b). One more thing, for an easier overview of the whole!

[p. 357] 1) Every circle expresses a complexion of relations, according to which everything in it, as a circle, is determined, and which yet do not lie in it as something afflicted with sensible marks (as something visible).

2) Every concept expresses a complexion of relations, according to which the marks of this concept are determined, and which yet do not lie in this concept as something afflicted with sensible marks.

3) The relations (from No. 1 and 2) are a thought, *as thought*, = (− B) = possibility.

4) That circle as well as the marks of this concept are (setting aside the material-containing in it) a thought, *as a thought something*, = (B) = actuality. Both together = (B − B) = object.

5) (B − B) produces necessarily a (+ b), i.e. the actuality, determined through the possibility, posits the form of the universal necessarily again and again on the form of the particular, gives accordingly a world-system, *as system*.

6) But (− B), or the possibility, at the head of a world-system, would be a negation at the head of a world-system.

[p. 358] 7) I *must* therefore go out beyond the world-system to a Plus which, what it is, already is *in and through* itself, to a Plus for the possibility of all other Plus, to a possibility of everything possible that falls into the world-system, to a being of first order, to an essence of essences, to a spirit of all spirits, to a primal substance for everything spiritual in the universe in general,—*to a God*.

8) A *modus inalterabilis* [unalterable mode] of his being κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν this God has revealed to me in my thinking; it is called *A as A in A, through A*, (law of identity).

9) If I relate everything in me to this *modus inalterabilis* of *his being* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν: then I follow truth, duty and right.

10) As the unchangeable and omnipresent God, which he is, he must therefore at the same time be the primal source of that which truth, duty and right is, must be *all-wise, all-good* and *just*.

11) All-wisdom, all-goodness and justice is consequently the rule according to which I have to judge the world-system, *as system* (*as thought*), according to which I have each of my experiences, *as experience* (not merely as sense-impression, intuition and φάντασμα), to sort [p. 359] out and *to understand*. Religiosity is my first duty with regard to the judging of the world; (σοφία *sapientia* of the Stoics, Off. 1, 43, *princepsque omnium virtutum est illa sapientia, quam* σοφίαν *Graeci nominant* [and chief of all the virtues is that wisdom which the Greeks call σοφία] l. c., *Plato* calls it νόησις. I am aware of no passage among the ancients through which Cicero’s *scientia rerum divinarum atque humanarum* [knowledge of things divine and human] l. c. would have become clearer to me than through the following saying of *Antonin’s Comment. ad s. i. Libr. III*, p. 37, “always, says he there, let the principles be present to you, according to which you can cognize the divine and the human, in order to accomplish everything, even the smallest, mindful of the connection between both; for without reference to the divine you will do nothing human aright, and conversely, without reference to the human nothing divine).”

12) *This kind* of religiosity will also become the surest basis of my morality; for it rests itself already on the recognition of that which is elevated above sensibility, as the highest, *indeed sole* reality: and only under this presupposition can it be expected of me to let myself be determined in my actions, *as man*, by that which *alone* has reality, but not by the sensible. If the supersensible has in itself no reality, [p. 360] but receives it first through intuitions, and one nevertheless expects of me to let myself be guided by it thereby: then one does not know what one wants.

13) What counts as ground of the apodictic certainty of these propositions (from No. 6), just that must likewise already be presupposed, if even only a logic, *as logic*, a doctrine of reason *from reason*, not merely from induction, is to be set up.

14) But he to whom it has now indeed been granted to be able to bear an intuition-philosophy, *as philosophy*, whether it have the Epicurean-French or the Kantian-German cut, hence, without any offense against his (quite peculiar) principle of contradiction, to transform the conditions on the side of the material into the cause, the ground, the *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, physics into a metaphysics, chemistry into an ontology, this physical metaphysics, this chemical ontology, however, once again (à la Kant) to declare for nothing less than for the attainment of what is *in itself* real, consequently to philosophize, without a philosophy ever emerging, and in this way alone *rightly* (critically) to philosophize—from him one must put up with it if he overturns for one, *together with all logic*, at once also all those propositions (from No. 6 on).

*Rem.* For the elucidation of the difference between mere conditions and the cause, the ground, the *prius* κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, the practical serves especially. Detention e.g. (*si quid manu teneo vel corpore occupo* [if I hold something with my hand or occupy it with my body]) can indeed be called a condition of appropriation, but is it therefore also its cause, etc.?
